# Korean — Established Historical-Linguistics Extraction: *A History of the Korean Language* (Part 2 of 2)

**Source:** Ki-Moon Lee & S. Robert Ramsey, *A History of the Korean Language* (Cambridge University
Press, 2011), 348 pp. This file (Part 2 of 2; Part 1 is `049_history_of_korean_language.md`, covering
the Introduction and Chapters 1–5.3) covers the **remainder of Chapter 5** — §5.4 Morphology, §5.5
Syntax, §5.6 Vocabulary, all still within "Late Middle Korean" (printed pp. 169–240, PDF pp. 181–252) —
plus **Chapter 6 "Early Modern Korean"** (printed pp. 241–286, PDF pp. 253–298) and **Chapter 7
"Contemporary Korean"**, the book's final chapter (printed pp. 287–305, PDF pp. 299–317). The book's
closing "Additional readings on selected topics," References, and Index (printed p. 306 onward) are
bibliographic/apparatus material, not language-reference content, and were deliberately out of scope
for this entire dispatch.

See Part 1 (`049_history_of_korean_language.md`) for the full source description, the project's
non-overlap rationale, the "Vocabulary"/"Grammar points" mapping convention used throughout both
files, and the extraction-quality caveats (Hangul syllable/jamo gaps, pitch-diacritic extraction
issues) that apply equally here. One additional, chapter-specific overlap note: **Chapter 7
"Contemporary Korean"** (the final section of this file) is the one part of this entire two-file
extraction that comes close to territory already covered by `033_lets_learn_korean_northern_dialect.md`
(a 1989 DPRK pedagogical primer documenting North/South phonological divergence from direct textual
evidence). That section's own text below states explicitly what was checked against 033 and kept as
non-redundant — in short, 033's synchronic word-pair evidence and this academic chapter's historical/
policy account of *why and when* that divergence happened turned out to be genuinely complementary
rather than overlapping, so nothing was skipped as redundant.

Section numbering below continues the source book's own chapter/section numbers (§5.4, §6.1, §7.1,
etc.) for easy cross-reference back to the original PDF.

---

## Korean — Established Vocabulary/Grammar: Late Middle Korean Morphology (I) — Word Formation, Nouns, Pronouns, Particles

**Source:** Ki-Moon Lee & S. Robert Ramsey, *A History of the Korean Language* (Cambridge University
Press, 2011), section 5.4 "Morphology" (within Chapter 5, "Late Middle Korean"), printed pp. 169–197
(PDF pp. 181–209). Covers the opening of 5.4 through the start of 5.4.3.2 "Stems"; a second subagent
covers the remainder of the chapter (printed pp. 198–226).

**Coverage note:** This is an academic diachronic monograph, not a teaching grammar — nearly every
cited form is a distinct lexical/morphological data point, not pedagogical filler. Given the sheer
density (dozens of suffixes, each illustrated with 3–10 example words), full exhaustive tabulation of
every single illustrative word in every list would produce an unusable table. Judgment call, stated
here for auditability: for suffix classes illustrated with a long list of near-identical examples
(e.g., the ~80 Middle Korean nouns ending in morphophonemic -h, the cardinal numeral set), a
representative subset is tabulated and the fuller list is named in the relevant Grammar Points prose;
every distinct *suffix/particle/grammatical morpheme* itself is captured, and every lexical item that
is the sole or primary illustration of a pattern is tabulated. Attested Era is uniformly "Late Middle
Korean (15th–16th c.)" per the source's own scope (a few items carry additional modern-dialect
comparative notes, flagged individually). Source has a clean, confirmed real text layer; no
vision-reading was required. No handwritten marginalia encountered.

| pilmek- | 'beg one's bread' | verb (compound) | archaic | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | p. 170 |

> **pilmek-** = pil- ("beg") + mek- ("eat") — direct stem-to-stem compounding, unbuffered; contrast
> Contemporary Korean's buffered pil.e mek- (pil- + infinitive -e/a + mek-). Illustrates that MK verb
> stems were more morphologically independent of inflectional endings than their modern reflexes (p. 170).

> **pswus-twolh** = pswuch- ("rub, grind") + twolh ("stone") — rare type: verb stem compounded
> directly with a noun (p. 170).

> **twuti-cwuy** = twuti- ("rummage, fumble") + cwuy ("rat") — verb stem + noun compound, "mole" (p. 170).

> **swutholk** = swuh ("male") + tolk ("chicken") — morphophonemic final -h aspirates the following
> plain obstruent (t→th); cf. **amtholk** = amh ("female") + tolk; **anphask** = anh ("inside") + pask
> ("outside"). This -h-triggered-aspiration rule is unproductive in Contemporary Korean noun
> compounding today, but its outputs survive as fossilized compounds (p. 171-172).

> **hanapi** = han- ("great," inflected modifier) + api ("father") — "grandfather"; compound built on
> an inflecting-stem modifier rather than a bare noun (p. 172).

> **nwoni-** = nwol- ("play, enjoy oneself") + ni- ("go," continuative) — "go around enjoying
> oneself." A whole micro-class of verbs is built on ni- as a bound continuative element (p. 172-173).

> **naza ka-** = nas-/naz- ("proceed") + ka- ("go") — modern-style infinitive-linked serial verb
> already attested in Middle Korean, alongside the older unbuffered type (p. 173).

---

### Grammar points

#### Inflected vs. uninflected word classes, and their blurring in Middle Korean

Contemporary Korean cleanly separates inflected words (verbs, adjectives, the copula — stem + ending,
neither usable standalone) from uninflected words (nouns, numerals, adverbs — which may optionally
take particles but aren't morphologically required to). In Late Middle Korean this distinction was
considerably less sharp: a number of nouns and verb/adjective stems shared an identical phonological
shape (poy/poy-, pis/pis-, etc.), and many adverbs likewise coincided with verb or adjective stems
(nowoy/nowoy-, ha/ha-, il/ilu-, etc.), without the derivational suffix (e.g., -i) Contemporary Korean
now requires to convert a stem into an adverb (p. 169-170).

#### Direct stem-to-stem verb compounding (unbuffered) vs. the modern infinitive-buffered pattern

In Late Middle Korean, combining two verb stems directly into a compound (pilmek- "beg one's bread")
was highly productive. Today this is normally buffered through the infinitive ending -e/a (pil.e
mek-). Direct compounding of a verb stem with a noun also occurred, though rarely attested
(pswus-twolh "grindstone," twuti-cwuy "mole"). The productivity of bare-stem verb compounding faded
after the sixteenth century, surviving only in a handful of fossilized forms (twolpwo-, selik-) — the
modern infinitive-buffered pattern (already attested in Middle Korean itself: naza ka-, twola wo-,
pese na-) became the only productive strategy (p. 170, 172-173).

#### The compound-vs-phrase boundary problem, including genitive-s compounds

Korean (both historical and modern) has no sharp dividing line between free-word compounds and
syntactic phrases; the deciding factor is usage/semantics (idiomatic flavor, frequency) rather than
structure — e.g. him psu- "endeavor" has verb-phrase structure (verb + direct object) but is
dictionary-treated as a compound when the object lacks its accusative particle (p. 171). A related
case is the "genitive s" particle (사이시옷): ordinarily a syntactic linker in noun phrases, but in
some constructions (mwoyskisulk "foot of a mountain," mulstolk "wild duck") the bonding is tight
enough that the construction is classed as a compound rather than a phrase, with the first element
functioning idiomatically as a prenoun (p. 171).

#### The morphophonemic -h aspiration rule in compounding

A noun ending in an underlying -h (e.g., swuh "male," amh "female," anh "inside") aspirates the
initial plain obstruent of a following compounded element: swuh + tolk → swu-tholk "rooster"; amh +
tolk → am-tholk "hen"; anh + pask → an-phask "inside and outside." The same rule applied in
verb-stem compounding (nah "age" + tul- "enter" → nathul- "grow old"). This rule is no longer
productive in Contemporary Korean noun compounding, but many of its MK outputs survive as lexicalized
relics (swuthalk, amthalk, anphakk) (p. 171-172).

#### Compound nouns: free-word, bound-element, and reduplicated types

The compound noun was (and remains) the most productive compound type. It could combine two or more
free words, or incorporate at least one bound element — a prenoun (woy "only" + pcak "member of a
pair" → woy-pcak "unmatched one of a pair"), an inflected modifier (han "great" + api "father" →
hanapi "grandfather"), or an inflecting stem (pswuch- "rub, grind" + twolh "stone" → pswus-twolh
"grindstone"). Adverb compounds followed the same pattern but favored reduplication more (na-nal
"daily," motoy-motoy "all joints," kaci-kaci "all kinds") (p. 171-172).

#### Serial verb-stem compounding with ni- 'go' as a continuative element

A distinct and productive micro-pattern compounded verb stems directly with ni- "go" as the second
element, functioning like a verbal suffix marking continuation of motion or action: nwoni- "go around
enjoying oneself" (< nwol- "play"), ketni-~kenni- "stroll, ramble" (< ket- "walk"), noni- "fly about"
(< nol- "fly"), etni- "keep looking for" (< et- "look for"), honi- "move" (< ho- "do, is"). This
pattern became unproductive after the sixteenth century, leaving only fossilized remnants like
twolpwo- "take care of" and selik- "become half-ripe" (p. 172-173).

#### The infinitive-plus-isi-/is- continuative-aspect construction and its contractions

The modern infinitive-buffered compounding pattern (-e/a attached to the first stem) is already
attested in MK (naza ka- "go ahead," twola wo- "return," pese na- "get out of"). A particularly common
elaboration of this pattern combines a verbal infinitive with the existential verb isi-/is- 이시-/잇-
functioning as an aspect marker indicating the continuation of a completed action, as in howoza anca
istesini "he was sitting alone" (1459 Wŏrin sŏkpo 1:6a). By the mid-fifteenth century this was already
commonly contracted to -ays/eys-, and by the sixteenth century further simplified to -as/es-. isi-/is-
could also contract further to 'si-/sy-. A set of exceptions drops the infinitive entirely: the verb
stem twu- "place" attaches isi-/is- or the honorific existential kyesi- directly, yielding forms like
twuysnwon, twukyesita, sometimes further contracted to twus- (p. 172-173).

#### Derivation via suffixation as the principal LMK word-formation process

Suffixation was the dominant derivational strategy in Late Middle Korean. A suffix attached to a word
or morpheme determined both the word class and the function of the resulting derived form (p. 173).
The remaining grammar points in this chunk work through the nominal, verbal, adjectival, and
adverbative suffix inventories in turn.

> **pwuhengi** = pwuheng ("owl," 1446 form) + -i (pleonastic nominal suffix) — one of three
> homophonous /i/-shaped LMK postposed elements (the others: the quasi-free noun i "one, person" and
> a deverbal nominalizer -i), illustrating how a single phonological shape /i/ bundled multiple
> historically related but functionally distinct nominal morphemes (p. 173-174).

> **ahoytol** = ahoy ("child") + -tolh ("and others," general plural) — unmarked pluralizer; contrast
> **emanimnay** = ema-nim ("respected mother") + -nay ("and other esteemed persons," honorific
> plural) — LMK had a three-way plural-marking split by register/animacy: general -tolh, honorific
> -nay, and pronoun-only -huy (p. 174-175).

> **twuyskan** = twuy ("rear") + -s- (linking) + kan ("room," Sino-Korean 間) — "toilet"; shows a
> Sino-Korean bound suffix combining productively with native vocabulary (p. 175).

#### Three homophonous /i/-shaped nominal elements

Late Middle Korean had (at least) three distinct postposed elements all sharing the phonological
shape /i/, probably from a single etymological source (and perhaps related to the homophonous subject
particle as well): (1) the quasi-free noun i "one, person," which could head an adnominalized
sentence and be lexicalized into compounds like nulkuni "old person"; (2) a deverbal nominalizer
-i that derives nouns from verb stems (discussed with verbal suffixes below); (3) a true nominal
suffix -i that attaches to nouns, seen in the shift from pwuheng → pwuhengi "owl," kulyek → kulyeki
"wild goose," phol → pholi "housefly," and likely already fossilized in api "father," emi "mother."
Because of this convergence, the source notes it's often difficult to tell which -i/-y at the end of
a Korean noun reflects incorporation of this particular suffix (p. 173-174).

#### Minor and obscure nominal-derivation suffixes: -ek/-ak, -wong, diminutives, vulgarizers, occupational and quality suffixes

A cluster of nominal suffixes with narrower or more obscure functions: -ek/-ak (thelek "hair,"
cwumek "fist," cwokak "bit," possibly kalak "toe/finger" though the base form is unattested); -wong
(kitwong "pillar"); the diminutive -(ng)aci (sywongaci "calf," kangaci "puppy") with a variant -yaci
lacking the velar nasal (moyaci "colt"); the diminutive -aki, itself derived from the noun aki "child"
(solaki "broken rice bits"); the vulgarizing -pak (melipak "head," tyengpak "crown of the head"); the
occupational -cyangi/tyengi "doer of..." (tamcyangi "mud wall builder," stuycyangi "beltmaker"); the
unit-noun -nach "piece, unit" (a doublet of the free noun nath), seen in solnach, samnas, pyesnach,
taysnach; and the quality-marking -pal, seen in pispal "streaks of rain," hoyspal "sun rays" (with
kulWal/kulwal "writing" analyzed as a lenited form of the same suffix) (p. 174).

#### Quasi-plural and honorific-plural suffixes

Late Middle Korean had several "quasi-plural" suffixes that, strictly, marked a noun as
*representative of a group* rather than marking grammatical plural number per se (p. 174, n.17). The
best-known, -tolh "and others," was a general unmarked pluralizer (ahoytol "children," POYKSYENGtol
"the people"). By contrast, -nay "and other esteemed persons" functioned specifically as an honorific
plural (ema-nim nay "the respected mothers"). A separate, *true* plural suffix -huy existed only for
pronouns (ne-huy "you people," ce-huy "we, humbly") — notably absent for first-person na, which
instead had the suppletive plural wuli "we." All of these pronoun plurals could in turn additionally
take -tolh (wuli-tolh, nehuy-tolh), confirming that -tolh's function was group-representativeness
rather than competing plural marking (p. 174-175).

#### Honorific and Sino-Korean nominal suffixes

The honorific suffix -nim "esteemed" (apa-nim "respected father," ema-nim "respected mother") derives
from the native noun nim "master." Many suffixes were imported from Chinese and mostly combine with
Sino-Korean vocabulary, but some combined with native stems too — e.g. Sino-Korean -kan 間 "room"
with native twuy "rear" to form twuys-kan "toilet." Some Sino-Korean-origin suffixes became
naturalized enough to obscure their Chinese source, e.g. -kyeng 景 "bright, view, scenery" in
mozoms-kyeng "state of mind" (p. 175).

> **yetulp-cahi** = yetulp ("eight") + -cahi (ordinal suffix) — "eighth." The suffix's basic form was
> -cahi; the aspirated variant -chahi is analyzed as arising via metathesis of a preceding numeral's
> final -h (e.g. seyh "three" + -cahi → sey-chahi "third"), though some aspirated forms occur even
> after non-h-final numerals (p. 176).

> **holo** ("one day") is reconstructed as deriving from *holol, incorporating a suffix *-(o/u)l also
> seen in ithul ("two days," suppletive stem ith- + -(u)l), saol/naol ("three/four days," with se/ne
> replaced by sa-/na- before the suffix), and yelhul ("ten days"). This reconstruction is picked up
> again in the historical noun-alternation discussion below (p. 176, 184-185).

> **yelum** vs. **yelwum**, both from yel- "bear fruit": the suffix -(o/u)m formed a true derivational
> split in Late Middle Korean that Contemporary Korean no longer preserves — a *lexical* noun was made
> by adding -(o/u)m directly to the bare stem (yel- + -um → yelum "fruit"), while a *sentential*
> nominalization (the verb functioning as a clause's predicate-turned-noun) additionally required the
> volitive infix -wo/wu- (yel- + wu- + -(u)m → yelwum "the bearing of fruit"). Both forms are attested
> together in a single period source passage: tywohon yelum yelwu.m_i "the bearing of good fruit"
> (1459 Wŏrin sŏkpo 1:12). A handful of sentential-type nominalizations later froze as ordinary lexical
> nouns despite retaining the volitive morpheme — chwum "dancing" (chu- + wu- + -m), wuzwum
> "laughing," wulwum "weeping" — showing the lexical/sentential split eroding even within the period
> (p. 176).

#### Nominalizing suffixes overview

Several suffixes converted verb or adjective stems into nouns. -(o/u)m was the most important,
deriving both ordinary lexical nouns (yelum "fruit," salom "person," kelum "step," kulim "painting,"
elum "ice") and sentential nominalizations (via an added volitive -wo/wu-, as in yelwum "bearing of
fruit" vs. yelum "fruit" from the same verb); a few sentential-type forms (chwum, wuzwum, wulwum)
became frozen as ordinary lexical nouns despite keeping the volitive morpheme. A rarer suffix -am/em
derived a few lexical nouns (mwutem "grave," cwukem "corpse," kwucilam/kwucilem "scolding"). The
suffix -i (homophonous with, but functionally distinct from, the two /i/-shaped nominal elements
discussed earlier) was remarkably productive for deriving nouns from verbs (wuzwumGwuzi "laughter,"
cwuksali "life and death," kulcizi "literary composition," hali "slandering"). -kay/key attached
similarly to verb stems to form instrument nouns (cipkey "tweezers," nolkay "wings," twupkey "lid";
etymologically also pwuchey "fan," kozGay "scissors," twulGey "circumference"). Although -ki is the
dominant nominalizer in the modern language, it was only rarely used in Middle Korean (patnonhwoki
"dividing the fields," kulsuki wa kalpsuki wa "wielding pen and sword"). Finally, -(o/u)y attached to
adjectives to form abstract-quality nouns: khuy "size," kiphuy "depth," nwophoy "height," nepuy
"width," kiluy "length" (p. 176-177).

#### Verbal suffixes: denominal verb formation (p. 177)

> **mwusk-** = mwus ("bundle") + -k- (denominal verbalizer) — "tie." A rare, narrowly-attested class
> of noun-to-verb derivation, distinct from the much larger causative/passive system discussed next
> (p. 177).

#### Causative suffixes: the four-way system (pp. 177–179)

Verbal/adjectival-stem-attaching suffixes split into causatives and passives. There were four
causative-forming suffixes, of sharply unequal productivity: (1) -hi/ki/Gi/i- (by far the most
common, its allomorph chosen by the preceding stem's final segment: -hi- after /p,t,c/, -ki- after
/m,s/, -Gi- after /z,l/, and -i-/-y- elsewhere); (2) -(G)wo/(G)wu- (second most productive, with an
underlying initial voiced velar fricative /G/, sometimes surfacing as -hwo/hwu- or -kwo- instead); and
two much rarer types, (3) -h- and (4) -o-. The two productive causatives, (1) and (2), could even
combine on a single stem, in either order (p. 177-179).

> **meki-** ("feed," causative of mek- "eat") vs. **mekhi-** ("be eaten," passive of the same verb) —
> the single most important minimal pair in this chapter's verbal morphology: causative and passive
> share the same suffix family (-hi/ki/Gi/i-), and mek- is the one documented case where the *same*
> verb takes visibly different allomorphs for its causative (-i-) vs. its passive (-hi-) reading (p.
> 177, 180).

> **epsiGwo-/epsiGwu-** = eps- ("not exist") + -i- (causative 1) + -Gwo-/-Gwu- (causative 2) —
> "eliminate, do away with." Demonstrates that the two productive causative suffixes could stack on a
> single stem, and in either order (contrast **alGwoy-** = al- + -Gwo- + -y-, the reverse stacking)
> (p. 179).

> **salGi-** ("cause to live in a place," sal- + -Gi-) vs. **salo-** ("save a life, let live," sal- +
> -o-) — same base verb, two different causative suffixes yielding genuinely different meanings, not
> free variants; the same contrastive pattern recurs with **ilGwu-** ("accomplish a goal," il- +
> -Gwu-) vs. **ilo-** ("erect a building," il- + -o-) (p. 179).

#### Passive suffix (pp. 179–180)

Passive stems were extremely rare in Middle Korean, just as in Contemporary Korean, and used the same
suffix family as causative (1): -hi/ki/Gi/i-. The sole documented case of a *different* allomorph
choice between a verb's causative and passive reading is mek- "eat": passive mekhi- "be eaten" uses
-hi-, while causative meki- "feed" uses -i-. Other passive examples: caphi- "get caught" (cap-
"grab"), telmki- "be dyed" (telm- "dye"), pwoy- "be seen" (pwo- "see"). A notable orthographic detail:
when forming passives on -y-final stems, the suffix's initial -Gi- was written with the special
double-zero symbol "ㆀ" — e.g. moyGi- "is bound to, by" (from moy- "bind, tie"), kwoyGye "being loved
by someone" (from kwoy- "love") (p. 179-180).

#### Adjectival suffixes (pp. 180–182)

Adjectival suffixes also split into two classes: those converting *nouns* into adjectives, and those
attaching to *inflecting stems* (verbs/adjectives). The most productive denominal adjectival suffix
was -toWoy- (later -towoy-), with allomorphs conditioned by the preceding segment and whether a
following suffix began with a vowel or consonant; it formed a doublet with -taW- (direct ancestor of
Contemporary Korean -tap/taw-), equally productive. A separate, older adjectival derivation is
preserved only as a trace in pulk- "reddish" and mulk- "watery," almost certainly derived from pul
"fire" and mul "water" at an earlier stage (p. 180-181).

> **alpho-** ("is hurt, sick") = alh- ("ail") + -Wo/Wu- (deverbal adjectival suffix, allomorph -o-
> after a consonant) — chosen as the illustrative case because the source notes most modern speakers
> no longer recognize the connection to the base verb, since the /l/ has elided in the derived
> adjective's modern reflex (alh- → al-, visible in alpho- vs. the expected *alhpho-) (p. 182).

> **askaW-** ("regrettable") = aski- ("spare, grudge") + -aW- — illustrates that suffix -aW/eW- only
> ever attached to i-final verb stems, with that final -i- regularly eliding; the one documented
> exception, **muzuyyeW-** ("is frightening," from muzuy- "be afraid of"), instead keeps the -i- as a
> glide -y- and the suffix develops a matching excrescent -y- (p. 182).

> **ili / kuli / tyeli** ("this way" / "that way" / "that way over there") = i/ku/tye (near/mid/far
> deictic pronouns) + -li (directional particle) — all three fossilized into ordinary lexical adverbs
> rather than remaining a live deictic+particle combination (p. 183).

> **towoyGey** = towoy- ("become") + -key (adverbative suffix, lenited to -Gey after a vowel/y-glide)
> — shows the regular lenition pattern (G-insertion) that recurs throughout this chapter's suffix
> system wherever a suffix-initial velar follows i/y (p. 184).

| hanolh | 'heaven' | noun (h-final, destabilizing) | core | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | representative example of the h-final class already showing instability within the 15th c. itself — attested both with (hanol.h_i) and without (hano.l_i) the final h (p. 184) |

> **twolh** ("stone") shows the full h-final paradigm: bare twol in isolation/before a consonant;
> twol.h- before a vowel-initial particle (twol.h_i "stone" + nominative, twol.h_ay "stone" +
> locative, twol.h_ol "stone" + accusative, twol.h_olwo "stone" + instrumental); and twol_khwa
> "stone" + comitative, where the h surfaces as aspiration of the particle's initial consonant rather
> than as [h] itself (p. 184).

#### Irregular (unpredictable) noun-shape alternation classes (pp. 184–185)

Beyond the predictable classes above, the source identifies four further alternation classes whose
behavior is not phonologically automatic, each historically explained by positing a lost final
consonant or cluster:

**Class 1 (velar-final, /namwo/~/namk/):** namwo "wood, tree" (nam.k_i, nam.k_ol before vowels, but
namwo_wa, namwo in isolation/before consonants) — reconstructed from earlier *namok, supported by
modern dialect comparative evidence (Pukch'ŏng nangk-, kwungk-, Kŏch'ang [puŋgu]). Also kwumwu
"hole," nyenu "other, different," pwulmwu "bellows" (p. 184-185).

**Class 2 (velar lenited to fricative, /nwolo/~/nwolG/):** nwolo "deer" (nwol.G_i, nwol.G_ol before
particles) — reconstructed from *nwolok, with *k lenited to /G/ rather than lost outright as in Class
1. Also nolo "ferry," silu "steamer," colo "handle," cyalo "sack" (p. 185).

**Class 3 (/z/-final, azo~azG):** azo "younger brother" (az.G_i, az.G_oy, az.G_ol before particles,
azo_wa in isolation-like contexts) — same type of alternation as Class 2 but conditioned after /z/.
Also yezu "fox," and probably mwuzwu "Korean radish, daikon" (p. 185).

**Class 4 (liquid-final, /molo/~/moll/):** molo "roof ridge" (mol.l_i, mol.l_oy, mol.l_ol) and holo
"one day" (same /holo/~/holl/ pattern) — reconstructed as *molol and *holol, involving /l/ rather than
a velar. *holol itself is further analyzed as incorporating the day-counting suffix *-(o/u)l discussed
above, ultimately traceable to a still-earlier *hotol (p. 185).

| Term | Gloss | Part of Speech | Usage Tier | Weight/Frequency | Attested Era | Attested Region | Geographic Scope | Source Type | Transcription Confidence | Vision Reading Confidence | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| namwo~namk | 'wood, tree' | noun (irregular alternation, Class 1) | core | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | Pukch'ŏng, Kŏch'ang (modern dialect comparative evidence) | regional | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | reconstructed *namok (p. 184-185) |
| nwolo~nwolG | 'deer' | noun (irregular alternation, Class 2) | core | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | reconstructed *nwolok, with lenited */k/ > /G/ (p. 185) |
| azo~azG | 'younger brother' | noun (irregular alternation, Class 3) | core | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | p. 185 |
| molo~moll | 'roof ridge' | noun (irregular alternation, Class 4) | archaic | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | reconstructed *molol (p. 185) |
| holo~holl | 'one day' | noun (irregular alternation, Class 4) | core | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | reconstructed *holol < *hotol; see also the day-counting -(o/u)l suffix discussion above (p. 185) |
| musu~musuk | 'what' | pronoun (irregular, tone-conditioned) | core | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | reconstructed *musuk; unlike Classes 1-4, the second-syllable vowel does NOT elide before a vowel-initial particle, because that syllable carries a HIGH tone (mùsú~mùsúk) rather than the low tone found in nàmwò/nwòlò-type nouns — a high-pitched vowel resisted the elision rule (p. 185) |

> **musu/musuk** ("what") is the chapter's key piece of *suprasegmental* evidence for these
> reconstructions: in all four irregular classes above, the elided/retained syllable carries a LOW
> tone, but musuk's second syllable is marked with a HIGH tone, and it is exactly this syllable that
> fails to elide before a vowel (musu.k_i, not *musu.y_i or similar) — showing tone, not just
> segmental shape, conditioned Middle Korean's vowel-elision rules (p. 185).

| aki~aka | 'child' (vocative) | noun (i-final elision) | core | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | final -i drops before vocative a (p. 186) |

> **say** ("new, fresh") and **nol** ("raw, unripe") illustrate a word-class demotion over time:
> Contemporary Korean restricts both to pre-nominal modifier (prenoun) position, but in Middle Korean
> they were ordinary free nouns able to stand alone or take case particles directly, e.g. say_lol
> maspwokwo "taste the new one, and..." (p. 186).

#### Pronoun morphology (pp. 186–188)

Pronoun morphology largely paralleled noun morphology, but with several distinctive quirks: notably
irregular pitch behavior, and the development of an excrescent -l with certain particles.

| Term | Gloss | Part of Speech | Usage Tier | Weight/Frequency | Attested Era | Attested Region | Geographic Scope | Source Type | Transcription Confidence | Vision Reading Confidence | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| na | 'I, me' | pronoun | core | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | low pitch in isolation; high before the topic particle (nán~nánón); high when the subject particle í is incorporated as a glide (náy); low when combined with genitive óy (này); rising before the object marker (năl~nălól) — four distinct tonal outcomes for one pronoun (p. 186-187) |
| ne | 'you (sg.)' | pronoun | core | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | low pitch in most environments (nè); low with genitive úy (nèy); rising when subject particle í is incorporated, since the particle's own high tone is preserved as a low+high sequence (nĕy) (p. 187) |
| nwu | 'who' | pronoun (interrogative) | core | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | high pitch with the subject particle (nwúy); rising with the genitive (nwŭy) (p. 187) |
| nwukwo/nwukwu | 'who' (fused interrogative) | pronoun (lexicalized fusion) | core | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | nwu + interrogative ending -kwo, fused and later (Early Modern period) lexicalized, replacing bare nwu in many usages (p. 187) |
| musum | 'what' (alternative form of musuk) | pronoun (interrogative) | core | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | p. 187 |
| enu | 'which' | pronoun/adverb (interrogative, dual function) | core | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | functioned as a full substantive in MK (enu_kwo "which is...?"), unlike Contemporary Korean's enu, now restricted to a prenoun modifier; also used adverbially as "how, why, in what way" (p. 187-188) |
| nallwo | 'as me' | pronoun (instrumental, excrescent -l) | core | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | pronouns developed an excrescent /l/ before the instrumental particle (o/u)lwo — na + lwo → nallwo, not *nalwo (p. 188) |
| nellwo | 'as you' | pronoun (instrumental, excrescent -l) | core | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | p. 188 |
| cellwo | 'with that, of its own accord' | pronoun (instrumental, excrescent -l) | core | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | already had the modern meaning "of its own accord, spontaneously" at this stage (p. 188) |
| nwullwo | 'as whom' | pronoun (instrumental, excrescent -l) | archaic | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | p. 188 |
| nwulGwa | 'with whom' | pronoun (accusative+comitative fusion) | archaic | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | object particle (l)o/ul + comitative fuses to -l_Gwa specifically after PRONOUNS (reverse order, -Gwa_lol, used after nouns) (p. 188) |
| nalGwa | 'and me' | pronoun (accusative+comitative fusion) | archaic | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | p. 188 |

> **nallwo** = na ("I") + -l- (excrescent) + -(o/u)lwo (instrumental) — "as me." Pronouns uniquely
> inserted an epenthetic /l/ before the instrumental particle that ordinary nouns did not; the same
> pattern recurs across the whole paradigm (nellwo "as you," illwo "with this," cellwo "with that,"
> nwullwo "as whom") (p. 188).

> **nwulGwa** = nwu ("who") + -l- (object particle, reduced) + -Gwa (comitative) — "with whom." When
> an object particle and the comitative particle combine after a PRONOUN, the order is
> object-then-comitative (-l_Gwa); after an ordinary NOUN the order reverses (comitative-then-object,
> (G)wa_lol) — a word-class-conditioned ordering asymmetry (p. 188).

#### Pitch as a pronoun-paradigm-distinguishing device

The pronouns na "I" and ne "you" show that Middle Korean's lexical tone system did real
morphosyntactic work at the pronoun level: the same base pronoun could carry up to three or four
distinct pitch contours depending purely on which particle was attached and how that particle's own
underlying tone interacted with the pronoun (incorporated as a full syllable vs. reduced to an
offglide). This is a clear instance of suprasegmental morphology distinguishing grammatical forms that
would otherwise be segmentally near-identical (na+i "high" vs. na+oy "low," both realized with the
vowel written ay/áy) (p. 186-187).

#### Particles: case particles vs. auxiliary particles (overview, p. 188)

Middle Korean particles split into two broad types, just as in the modern language: (1) "case
particles" (grammarians' term), which mark the syntactic role of the noun/noun phrase they attach to;
and (2) "special particles" (특수조사) or "auxiliary particles" (보조조사), a broader category covering
emphasis, focus, attitude, emotion, and intent. Korean grammarians recognize seven cases marked by
particles: nominative, accusative, genitive, locative-allative, instrumental, comitative, and
vocative (p. 188).

#### Nominative particle i and the first attestation of ka (pp. 188–189)

| Term | Gloss | Part of Speech | Usage Tier | Weight/Frequency | Attested Era | Attested Region | Geographic Scope | Source Type | Transcription Confidence | Vision Reading Confidence | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| i (subject particle) | nominative marker | particle | core | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | /i/ after consonants, reduced to offglide /y/ after vowels; raises a low-pitched syllable's tone to rising when incorporated as an offglide, no effect if the syllable was already tonic (p. 188) |
| ka (subject particle) | nominative marker, emergent | particle | core | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | 16th c., letter attributed to the mother of the poet Chŏng Ch'ŏl | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | absent from all 15th-c. texts; earliest known attestation is a 1572 private letter — direct textual evidence that ka entered Korean by at least the latter half of the 16th c. (p. 188-189) |

> **ka**'s 1572 attestation (Chon kwutoloy cani poy_ka seynilesye colwo tonnini, "Having slept on a
> cold floor, my stomach hurt, so I had to go a lot") is treated by the source as the earliest solid
> textual evidence for this now-ubiquitous nominative particle — notably from an informal private
> letter rather than a printed religious or scholarly text, a genre gap worth flagging for this
> project's purposes since vernacular/informal registers are exactly where innovations like this
> surface first (p. 188-189).

#### Subject-marking optionality

As in Contemporary Korean, marking the subject (or other grammatical roles) with a case particle was
never obligatory in Middle Korean — many subjects appear unmarked in the textual corpus, and the same
was true of other case particles generally. The source notes the choice to mark or omit probably
carried semantic implications similar to today's, though this is difficult to establish with
certainty from the textual record alone (p. 189).

#### Accusative particle -(l)ol/ul (p. 189)

The accusative particle -(l)ol/ul marked direct objects. After a consonant-final noun it took the
vowel-harmony-governed shape ol/ul (musu.k_ul "which one," ptu.t_ul "intent"); after a vowel it was
frequently reduced to just /l/ (kachi_l "magpies [object]," hanapi_l "Grandfather [object]"),
especially common when marking pronouns (wulil "us," nwul "whom," nal "me"). A separate, fuller form
lol/lul also occurs after vowels (pwuthye_lul "the Buddha," na_lol "me") and is analyzed as a doubling
of the particle (p. 189).

#### Genitive particles: oy/uy vs. -s, and the honorific/animacy split (pp. 189–190)

Middle Korean had two genitive particles with a functional split along animacy and honorific lines.
oy/uy was used with animates (people, animals) as a plain, non-honorific marker. The genitive -s
(이시옷, "medial s") had a dual function: with people, it served as an *honorific* marker; with
inanimates, it was simply the ordinary genitive. A single period-source passage neatly contrasts the
two: cokyas wosolan paskwo [...] KKWUTTAM_oy wo.s_ol nipusya — Sakyamuni's own clothing is marked with
honorific -s (cokya-s, since it refers to Sakyamuni) while his servant Kudam's clothing is marked with
plain oy (KKWUTTAM-oy), in the very same sentence (1459 Wŏrin sŏkpo 1:5b) (p. 189). Separately,
genitive oy/uy was a homophone of the *locative* marker oy/uy, distinguished purely by animacy
context: genitive-oy/uy only with animates, locative-oy/uy only with inanimates (p. 189).

| -s (genitive, "medial s") | inanimate genitive / honorific-animate genitive | particle | core | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | 사이시옷; dual function split by animacy+status rather than a single uniform meaning (p. 189) |
| nalas SYWOWMIN | 'the common people of the country' | noun phrase | archaic | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | -s as plain inanimate genitive (p. 189) |
| SYEYCWON_s SSINLUK | 'the holy power of Sakyamuni' | noun phrase | archaic | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | -s as honorific genitive on a person (p. 189) |

#### Locative particles and the dative construction built on the genitive (pp. 190–192)

Two basic locative-allative particles, ay/ey and oy/uy, were both restricted to inanimate nouns; the
difference between them is "still obscure" per the source, though oy/uy tended to attach to a fixed
lexical set. ay/ey's shape followed vowel harmony (ay after yang vowels, ey after yin vowels, yey
after i/y). A striking irregularity: following certain tonic monosyllabic nouns (and at least one
dissyllabic noun, swòlí "sound"), ay/ey caused the preceding noun's high pitch to LOWER (nwún "eye" +
éy → nwù.n_éy, not *nwú.n_éy) — but this lowering was lexically specific, not general (múl "water" +
éy → mú.l_éy, no lowering). Modern Hamgyŏng-dialect reflexes are cited as partial comparative
confirmation of this tonal quirk (p. 190).

Because locative-allative particles were restricted to inanimates, DATIVE marking (locative sense
with animates) required a separate, transparently-compositional construction built on the genitive
particle: plain-animate dative = genitive oy/uy + key/kungey/kekuy/swontoy; honorific dative =
genitive -s + key/kuy/kungey/kekuy. The grammatical elements key, kungey, kekuy themselves derive from
noun phrases meaning "to/in that place" — ku "that" + ngekuy (itself from kekuy "to a place," which
already contains the locative-allative uy) → ku.ngekuy → contracted ku.ngey → further contracted to
the single-syllable key. The other deictics i "this" and tye "that over there" formed parallel
chains: i_ngekuy (contracted to yekuy in period texts) and tye_ngey (< *tye_ngekuy) (p. 190-191).

> **ku.ngekuy → ku.ngey → key**, all glossed "to/in that place," is the chapter's clearest
> grammaticalization chain: a full noun phrase (deictic ku "that" + locational noun ngekuy, itself
> historically kekuy "to a place" + the locative particle uy) progressively phonologically reduces
> across three attested stages down to a single bound-feeling syllable key, which synchronically
> functions as part of the dative-marking system (p. 190-191).

> **tolye** = toli- ("takes a person along with one") + infinitival -ye — grammaticalized from a full
> verb into a dative-marking adverbial particle, used together with (not instead of) the accusative
> particle; cf. **tepule**, the same process on tepul- ("take a person with"), restricted to marking
> an inferior as the recipient (p. 192).

#### Instrumental, comitative, and vocative case particles (pp. 192–193)

The instrumental particle (o/u)lwo marked a broad range of senses — instrument proper, causality,
role, and directionality — e.g. kal.h_olwo "with a knife," CWOY_lwo "because of a transgression"
(1459 Wŏrin sŏkpo 1:6b), cey nala.h_olwo kal cce.k_uy "when going to one's own country" (1459 Wŏrin
sŏkpo 2 6:22) (p. 192).

The comitative particle ("with, and, accompanying") had two orthographic/phonological forms: 과 (kwa)
and 와. 와 represented plain wa after a vowel, but /Gwa/ (voiced velar initial) after /l/ — mol_Gwa
"horses and..." vs. sywo_wa "oxen and...". Starting in the early sixteenth century, kwa began
displacing Gwa after /l/, becoming the norm by the century's second half (hoyngsil_kwa, mal_kwa in the
1577 Kyech'o simhak inmun; swol_kwa, kulwel_kwa in the 1587 Sohak ŏnhae). The same late-16th-century
texts also show kwa spreading to follow /y/ (syenpoy_kwa, eskey_kwa), a usage that persisted briefly
into the Early Modern period before kwa was replaced by wa in that environment as diphthongs
monophthongized (p. 192-193).

The three vocative particles were register-differentiated by relative rank: ha (honorific — used by a
lower-ranked speaker addressing a superior), a (used by a superior addressing an inferior), and ya/ye
(a third, exclamatory-flavored vocative). The source's illustrative example is a single narrative
scene showing both register-marked particles in use: a king addressing Sakyamuni, or retainers
addressing the king, say SYEYCWON_ha "O Sakyamuni" / TTAYNGWANG_ha "O Great King" (honorific ha), but
when Sakyamuni addresses the king he says TTAYNGWANG_a "O King" (plain a) — the same addressee term,
different particle, by relative rank of speaker to addressee (p. 193).

| Term | Gloss | Part of Speech | Usage Tier | Weight/Frequency | Attested Era | Attested Region | Geographic Scope | Source Type | Transcription Confidence | Vision Reading Confidence | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| (o/u)lwo (instrumental) | 'with, by means of, because of, to(ward)' | particle | core | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | p. 192 |
| wa/kwa (comitative) | 'and, with, accompanying' | particle | core | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | kwa gradually displaced the older Gwa-after-/l/ and (briefly) kwa-after-/y/ variants over the 16th c. (p. 192-193) |
| ha (vocative) | honorific vocative, inferior-to-superior | particle | archaic.formal | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | p. 193 |
| a (vocative) | plain vocative, superior-to-inferior | particle | archaic | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | p. 193 |
| ya/ye (vocative) | exclamatory vocative | particle | archaic | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | MWUNSYWUW SOLI_ye! "Oh Munjusari!" (p. 193) |

> **SYEYCWON_ha** ("O Sakyamuni") vs. **TTAYNGWANG_a** ("O King") — the same narrative scene uses two
> different vocative particles on different addressees purely by relative social rank of speaker to
> addressee: ha when a lower-status speaker addresses a higher-status one, a in the reverse
> direction. This is a vocative-particle-level instance of the honorific stratification that recurs
> throughout LMK's case-particle system (cf. the genitive -s split and the dative constructions
> above) (p. 193).

#### Auxiliary ("special") particles (pp. 193–196)

Auxiliary particles (특수조사/보조조사) express emphasis, focus, contrast, restriction, and similar
notions rather than syntactic case role. The source works through roughly eighteen of these in turn;
summarized by function below, with the vocabulary table capturing each particle as a distinct
grammatical-morpheme entry.

**Topic marking — (n)on/un, and its accusative-fused form (o/u)lan:** The topic particle was on/un
after consonants; after vowels it was often reduced to bare -n, or doubled as non/nun (the more common
shape when attached directly to a noun; bare -n was more common when the topic particle instead
followed another particle — e.g. locative ay/ey + topic → ayn/eyn; instrumental + topic → ulwon;
comitative + topic → wan/kwan; dative + topic → kungen, kekuyn). When the topic particle instead
followed the ACCUSATIVE, the fused result was (o/u)lan: cey psol_lan kochwokwo "storing up his own
rice..." (1459 Wŏrin sŏkpo 1:45a-b) (p. 193-194).

**two 'too, also, even':** Functioned much like its modern reflex, replacing i or (l)ol/ul, but often
instead following another particle (ey_two, lwo_two) (p. 194).

**man 'only, just' and spwun 'only, merely, just':** man's distribution paralleled two, with which it
formed a semantic contrast (exclusive vs. inclusive focus); spwun differed in being combinable with
nominative, accusative, and locative particles (p. 194).

**puthe '(starting) from' and skocang 'up until':** puthe originated as the infinitive of the verb
puth- "append" — following the accusative, it indicated origin/cause; following the instrumental, a
starting point. skocang originated as genitive -s + the noun kocang "limit," meaning either "to the
full extent of" or "up until" (p. 194).

**twukwo and lawa, both 'than':** twukwo, used in comparisons (functionally the ancestor-equivalent
of modern pwota), derived transparently from the verb twu- "put, place"; in the 16th c. it commonly
fused with the topic marker as twukwon. lawa, also comparison-marking, has an obscure etymology and
disappears from the textual record after the 16th century entirely (p. 195).

**sye 'from, at':** Derived from the infinitive of the existential verb isi- "exist, be"; marked
origin or dynamic location, and could attach to nouns, adverbs, other particles (puthe, directional
lwo), or verbal forms (p. 195).

**cwocha 'even, too':** The infinitive form of verb cwoch- "chase after"; following the accusative it
meant "accompanying, going with," following the noun directly it meant "even, too, in addition" (p.
195).

**taWi/tahi, and its reflex taylwo, 'in accordance with':** Believed to derive from an unattested
verb stem *taW- "resemble, be like." The form shifted to tai by the late 15th c., and from tai + lwo
the modern postposition taylwo descends — notably, taylwo itself is already attested within the
Middle Korean corpus, not purely a later development (p. 195-196).

**iston 'just, precisely, only':** Believed (not certainly) to derive from a copula form (p. 196).

**Emphatic za (later ya):** The most widely used LMK emphatic particle, with a highly idiosyncratic
distribution — following unmarked subjects/objects/adverbs ending in /i/ or /y/, time nouns ending in
/l/, or after the nominative, accusative, instrumental, or locative particles, and after several
inflectional endings (prefinal -ke-, final -a/e, -nul, -tun, -kwo, -key). za was progressively replaced
by ya starting in the latter half of the 16th century — confirmed by a bilingual Chinese/Korean
parallel passage in the 1587 Sohak ŏnhae where the Chinese text's particle corresponds to Korean za,
suggesting the actual period pronunciation had already shifted to [ya] (p. 196-197).

**Emphatic -k:** Attached mainly after the infinitive -a/e, and also after the instrumental (o/u)lwo
(p. 197).

**Emphatic kwos/Gwos and pwos/pos:** Both attached directly to nouns/adverbs with similar emphatic
meaning; kwos surfaced as Gwos after /l/ or a vowel (including /y/); the conditioning for pwos/pos's
vowel alternation is unknown (p. 197).

**Distributive kwom/Gwom, later replaced by sik, both 'each':** kwom/Gwom attached to numerals/nouns
meaning "each"; Gwom after vowels/l. It also had an unrelated intensifying function when attached to
certain adverbs (see hoyyekwom below). sik first appears in alphabetic (Hangul) documents only in the
16th century, but its presence in idu transcription (as 式) as early as the 1395 Taemyŏngnyul chikhae
shows it must already have existed in actual 15th-century speech, predating its alphabetic attestation
(p. 197-198... continues to next chunk's p. 198).

**(i)ye 'or, and, and the like,' and the related ending -mye:** Used in listing two or more items; also
realized as ya in the 16th century. The modern inflectional ending -mye ("does/is and") is analyzed as
historically composed of the nominalizing suffix -(o/u)m plus this particle ye — supported by the fact
that -mye was correspondingly realized as -mya in the same 16th-century texts where ye→ya (p. 198,
continues into next chunk).

| pwos/pos | emphatic particle | particle | archaic | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | vowel-alternation conditioning unknown (p. 197) |
| kwom (intensifying) | intensifying particle on adverbs | particle | archaic | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | distinct function from distributive kwom below; hoyyekwom "letting, making, forcing," the ancestor of Contemporary hayekum (p. 197-198) |
| kwom/Gwom (distributive) | 'each' | particle | core | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | on numerals/nouns; later replaced by sik (p. 197) |
| sik | 'each' | particle | core | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | first alphabetic attestation 16th c.; idu precedent (式) from 1395 shows it existed in speech earlier (p. 197) |

> **hoyyekwom** = hoy- (causative of ho- "do, is") + -ye (adverbial/infinitive) + kwom (intensifying
> particle) — "letting, making, forcing..."; the direct ancestor of Contemporary Korean's fossilized
> hayekum, and a good example of a whole multi-morpheme construction surviving into the modern
> language as a single opaque unit (p. 178, 197-198).

> **twukwon** = twukwo ("than," from twu- "put") + -(n)on (topic marker), fused in 16th-century usage
> — illustrates that these auxiliary particles kept combining productively with the core particle
> system (here the topic marker) rather than being fixed, non-combinable units (p. 195).

#### Conjugations: verbs and adjectives share morphology, with -no- as the classifying test (pp. 198–199)

| Term | Gloss | Part of Speech | Usage Tier | Weight/Frequency | Attested Era | Attested Region | Geographic Scope | Source Type | Transcription Confidence | Vision Reading Confidence | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| -no- (prefinal ending) | processive-aspect marker, verb-only | affix (inflectional, prefinal) | core | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | the diagnostic test used to classify an inflecting stem as a VERB rather than an adjective, since almost all other endings attached to both classes identically (p. 198) |
| is- | 'be, exist, have' (existential) | verb | core | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | classed as a VERB specifically because it occurred with -no- (p. 198) |
| eps- | 'not exist, there is not' (negative existential) | adjective | core | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | classed as an ADJECTIVE because it did NOT occur with -no- at this period, unlike its positive counterpart is- (p. 198) |
| polk- | 'is bright' / 'become bright' (dual-category stem) | adjective/verb (dual-function) | core | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | an inflecting stem that functioned as BOTH word classes: adjectival "is bright," but also usable with the processive -no- in the verbal, inchoative sense "become bright" (kwulu.m_i huthwu.m_ay tol_i polknoni, "diffused by clouds, the moon brightened," 1564 Sŏn'ga kwigam 1:39) (p. 198) |

> **eps-** ("not exist") vs. **is-** ("exist") is the chapter's clean minimal pair demonstrating the
> -no- diagnostic: these two near-opposite existential stems are grammatically classed into different
> word classes (verb vs. adjective) purely because one of them combined with the processive prefinal
> ending -no- at this period and the other did not — not because of any obvious semantic or
> morphological markedness difference between them (p. 198).

> **polk-** ("is bright" / "become bright") shows that the verb/adjective distinction was not always
> fixed per stem: the same stem could function adjectivally (stative "is bright") or verbally
> (processive "become bright," licensed by -no-) depending on the intended reading, with -no-'s
> presence or absence serving as the surface signal of which reading was meant (p. 198).

#### Verbs and adjectives: near-identical morphology, the inherited default

As in Contemporary Korean, Late Middle Korean verbs and adjectives shared virtually the same
inflectional morphology — almost every inflectional ending attached indifferently to both stem
classes. The sole systematic exception was the prefinal ending -no- (processive aspect), which
attached to verb stems only, making its presence/absence the standard criterion modern grammarians use
to classify a given LMK stem as verbal rather than adjectival (p. 198).

#### Stems: typical shape and the segmental/suprasegmental alternation split (p. 199, opening)

The typical Middle Korean inflecting stem was monosyllabic; two-syllable stems were also fairly
common, but anything longer than two syllables was invariably a compound (not a simplex stem). Stem
shapes often alternated depending on the attached inflectional ending, and these alternations split
into two broad types: **segmental** alternations, derived by automatic phonological rules applying to
a single underlying phonemic shape (e.g., before a consonant-initial ending, W and z neutralized to p
and s respectively, and consonant clusters reduced) — and a second, more complex category the source
begins to introduce at the exact point this chunk's page range ends (p. 199). **This second
category — along with the rest of 5.4.3 "Conjugations" — is covered by the next subagent's chunk
(printed pp. 198–226).**

## Korean — Established Vocabulary/Grammar: Late Middle Korean Morphology, Part 2 (Stem Alternations, Tone Classes, the Copula, and Inflectional Endings)

**Source:** Ki-Moon Lee & S. Robert Ramsey, *A History of the Korean Language* (Cambridge University Press, 2011), section 5.4 "Morphology," within chapter 5 "Late Middle Korean," printed pp. 198–226 (PDF pp. 210–238 of the scanned file). This chunk is the second half of the chapter's morphology section; a sibling chunk covers printed pp. 169–197.

**Coverage note:** This is a diachronic academic monograph, not a teaching grammar — its "vocabulary" is almost entirely the illustrative 15th–16th-century verb/adjective stems the authors cite while laying out Middle Korean's stem-alternation and tone classes, plus the grammatical morphemes (endings) that make up the rest of the inflectional system. Every stem example and every named ending in this page range is captured below; nothing here is a repeated drill — each item is cited by the source exactly once as a distinct illustration. Clean text layer (`pdftotext`, no cipher, no vision-reading needed); Hangul, Yale romanization, and rare Hanja glosses are preserved as printed. Where the same stem recurs across two subsections (e.g., as both a stem-class example and a tone-class example), it is listed once with both page citations rather than duplicated, except where the second occurrence reveals a genuinely distinct inflected form (e.g., a stem's low(–low) paradigm given later in the chapter) — those are kept as separate rows and cross-referenced.

All items below are historical (15th–16th century) forms; **Usage Tier is `archaic` throughout** (none of these survive as such in Contemporary Korean, though many have regular modern reflexes noted in the Notes column). **Attested Era is `Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.)` throughout** per this book's own periodization. No regional/dialectal marking occurs in this page range, so Attested Region/Geographic Scope are `—` throughout.

---

### Vocabulary

#### W-stems and z-stems (p. 198)

Middle Korean stems ending in the voiced fricatives /W/ (ㅱ) or /z/ (ㅿ). These fricatives were realized phonemically only before vowels; before a consonant they were replaced by /p/ and /s/ respectively.

#### Tone classes of monosyllabic stems (pp. 200–204)

Every Middle Korean inflecting stem carried an inherent pitch pattern — this is treated by the source as part of the stem's morphology because the pattern is unpredictable from segmental shape alone and must be learned stem-by-stem.

> **chiW-** = chi- (bound root, cf. chó- 'be cold to the touch') + -Wu- (adjective-deriving prefinal ending) (p. 201 n.21; repeated here for cross-reference).

> **koth(o)-** = kot (bound noun, 'the fact of being like —', attested in compounds) + ho- ('do, be') — the authors cite this as the reason koth- behaves as morphemically complex despite its surface shape as a simple high-tone CVC stem (p. 201 n.21).

> **nah(o)-** = na- ('come out, be born,' intransitive) + -h(o)- (causative suffix, itself probably a reduced form of ho- 'do') — "give birth to" is literally "cause to come out/be born" (p. 202).

> **muyp- / muywu-** = muy- ('hate,' bound root) + -Wu- (prefinal ending forming low(–low)-pattern adjectives, the same morpheme analyzed in moyp-/moywo- above) (p. 202–203).

#### Longer (dissyllabic and trisyllabic) stems (p. 204)

Dissyllabic stems typically ended in l, y, or i with a second-syllable vowel o, u, or i, and carried a low–high tone pattern; stems longer than two syllables were generally compounds, with a handful of exceptions carrying a morphophonemic third-syllable vowel.
