#### The copula and a key nominalization example (p. 204–206, 214–215)

| Term | Gloss | Part of Speech | Usage Tier | Weight/Frequency | Attested Era | Attested Region | Geographic Scope | Source Type | Transcription Confidence | Vision Reading Confidence | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| i- | is (copula) | copula | archaic | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | predicator attaching to a noun/noun phrase; phonologically /iy/, not simple /i/ — see the Copula grammar point below for its idiosyncratic behavior (p. 204–205) |
| elGwun | adult | noun | archaic | — | Late Middle Korean (15th-16th c.) | — | — | grammar_reference | n/a | n/a | rare example of a 15th-c. deverbal noun formed directly with the modifier/nominalizer -(o/u)n; this kind of nominalization had already stopped being productive by the 16th c. (p. 214) |

> **elGwun** "adult" = {verb root, 'be grown/adult'} + -(o/u)n (modifier ending, used here as a nominalizer) — one of the few fossilized nouns showing the fifteenth-century use of the modifier ending -(o/u)n as a direct noun-forming suffix, a usage no longer productive by the sixteenth century (p. 214).

---

### Grammar points

#### Stem-alternation classes overview

Middle Korean verb/adjective stems were not phonologically uniform the way most Contemporary Korean stems are; several distinct classes alternated their final consonant or vowel depending on whether the following ending began with a vowel or a consonant. Lee and Ramsey catalogue seven such classes in this stretch of text (p. 198–200): **W-stems** and **z-stems** (voiced fricatives realized only before vowels, replaced by /p/ and /s/ before consonants); **cluster-final stems** (-sk-, -st-, -mch-, -ps-, -nc-, reduced before consonants by sk→s, st→s, mch→ms, ps→p, nc→ns); **G-stems and k-stems** (an unexpected velar appears before vowel-initial endings in three small classes: simu-/simk-, talo-/talG-, pozo-/pozG-); **t/l stems** (dental stop before consonants, liquid before vowels — still true in the standard language today); **l-dropping stems** (-l suppressed before the coronals t, c, s, z, n); **l-doubling stems** (final vowel drops and /l/ doubles before certain vowel-initial endings, e.g. mwolo- → mwolla); and **l-inserting stems** (the final vowel never drops; instead an extra -l- is inserted — in Middle Korean this class had only one true member, nilul-/nilu- 'reach', with today's three-member class (nwulu-, phwulu-, ilu-) emerging later from the loss of a semantic distinction between process/state verb pairs).

#### Vowel elision and crasis

A stem-final minimal vowel (o/ㆍ or u/ㅡ) elided before another vowel, following fixed rules: a+a→a, e+e→e, o+a→a, u+e→e, o+wo→wo, u+wu→wu, i+i→i (p. 200). Separately, a stem-final -i- became -y- before a, e, wo, or wu (neki- + -e → nekye), and a stem-final -y- induced an excrescent y- before the same vowels (yehuy- → yehuyye). The verb ho- 'is, does' was the sole irregular exception, behaving as though it carried an inherent i/y vocalism (infinitive hoya, volitive usually hoywo-, though the regularly-formed hwo- is also attested) (p. 200).

#### Irregular existential/honorific stem contractions

Beyond the regular alternation classes, three specific stems showed idiosyncratic shortenings: nye- 'travel about' → ni- before the perfective -ke-; the honorific existential kyesi- 'is' → kye- before the honorific imperative -sywosye (revealing that -si- here is historically the honorific morpheme -(o/u)si-); and the ordinary existential isi- 'is' → is- before most consonant-initial endings, with a further contraction to 'si-/sy- after the infinitive -a/e or after a word ending in -i (p. 200–201).

#### Tone classes of inflecting stems

Every Middle Korean monosyllabic stem carried a lexically fixed tone pattern, independent of its segmental shape, that the authors treat as part of its morphology: **Low** (the majority type, closed by a consonant/cluster/-y, e.g. mek- 'eat'); **High** (two subtypes — closed by -l/-m/-y, or open-syllable with an initial cluster/aspirate, the latter believed to derive historically from syncope of a low minimal vowel, e.g. *pusu- > psu- 'use'); **Rising** (~40 stems, mostly y-final, some derived with the passive morpheme -i-, e.g. syey- 'stand up' from sye-); **Irregular** (the (C)V- stems, whose tone shifts unpredictably by ending — pwo- 'see' and sye- 'stand' are given as full worked examples); **Low/rising** (~100 voiced-consonant-final stems alternating low before a vowel and rising before an obstruent-initial ending, e.g. teW-~tep- 'be hot' — reconstructed from an earlier high minimal vowel *teWu- whose loss produced the alternation); **Low(–low)** (~80 stems with a low-pitched second syllable, including the h-stem causatives like nah(o)-, several voiced-consonant-plus-minimal-vowel pairs like tot-/tolo-, the G/k-stem class, and the regular l-doubling stems — with mwolo- 'not know' and nwulu- 'press' as tonal exceptions within that last group); and the **longer-stem** pattern (dissyllabic stems typically low–high, pp. 200–204). The authors stress there was "no obvious phonological conditioning" for several of these alternations (p. 202) — tone class had to be memorized per stem, much like gender in European languages.

#### The copula i-

The copula i- 'is' attached to a preceding noun/noun phrase with tight juncture, transcribed identically to the nominative particle i, but its phonological shape was actually /iy/, not simple /i/ — demonstrated by the fact that it triggered velar lenition (k→G) in the endings -ke-, -key, -kwo exactly as /y/ regularly did elsewhere (e.g., TYEN-CO_iGesini 天子ㅣ어시니 'it was the Son of Heaven') (p. 204–205). Two further copula-specific changes are unexplained etymologically: an initial /t/ in the endings -te-, -two-, -ta became /l/ after the copula (e.g., PEP_ila 法이라 'it is the law'), and the volitive -wo- became -lwo- after the copula (hon kaci_lwom_ol 가지로), with 15th-century texts already showing occasional loss of that /l/ (-wo- directly). Beyond its modern role as a noun-predicator, the Middle Korean copula also attached productively to prefinal and conjunctive endings in constructions whose meaning is only partly understood today (e.g., ati mwotkeyla 아디 몯게라 'one cannot know') (p. 205).

#### Inflectional endings: overview

Middle Korean inflectional morphemes split into **prefinal endings** (medial-position morphemes marking intention, politeness, tense/aspect, and emotive force) and **final endings** (word-final morphemes marking modality/function — declarative, linking to other clauses, nominalizing, modifying a noun, etc.) (p. 205).

#### Prefinal: Volitive -wo/wu-

Called the "modulator" by Martin (1992), this morpheme's meaning is described as "enigmatic." After a consonant-final stem it alternated -wo-/-wu- by vowel harmony; after o/u/i it followed regular elision/y-insertion rules; after a/e/wo/wu it elided outright, leaving only a tonal trace. It combined idiosyncratically with other morphemes: retrospective -te- + volitive → -ta-; honorific -(o/u)si- + volitive → -(o/u)sya-; copula i- + volitive → -ilwo- (p. 205–206). Usage was syntactically conditioned rather than free: obligatory before the nominalizer -(o/u)m and the adverbial -toy; forbidden before endings like -kwo, -kuy, -twolwok, -(o/u)myen, -a/e; and, before the modifier endings -(o/u)n/-(o/u)lq, present only when the modified noun was the semantic object of the adnominalized verb (cizwon CCWOY 지은 罪 'sins that one committed' vs. cizun mwom 지은 몸 'a body that has committed [a sin]', where 'body' is not the object) (p. 206–207). Semantically it marked subjective will/intent rather than factual narration — speaker's intent in declaratives, addressee's intent in questions. The morpheme began destabilizing in the fifteenth century and had fallen out of use by the end of the sixteenth, in two documented stages: first (early 16th c.) it stopped appearing in environments where it was optional, then (late 16th c.) it dropped even from environments where it had been obligatory, as shown by the Sohak ŏnhae (1587) nominalizations ancom, epsom, mekum, psum lacking the volitive that 15th-century forms would have required (p. 206–207).

#### Prefinal: Honorific -(o/u)si-

Added to a verb/adjective to show respect for the grammatical subject (p. 207). It had an underlyingly low-pitched initial vowel, and combined with the volitive to give -(o/u)sya- (also realized this way before the infinitive -a/e). This -(o/u)sya form fell out of use in the sixteenth century, replaced by -(o/u)sye, as attested already in the 1518 Iryun haengsil to (p. 207–208).

#### Prefinal: Deferential -zoW-

Expressed deference toward the person *affected by* the action (exalting the object, the mirror image of subject-honorific -(o/u)si-) — e.g., used when commoners act toward the Buddha or king (p. 208). Its shape varied by the preceding consonant: -soW- after k/p/s/h, -zoW- after a vowel/n/m, -coW- after t/c/ch (the apical-stop-plus-/z/ sequence surfacing as the affricate written ㅈ); its final -W- devoiced to -p- before a consonant. Its tone also varied conditionally on the preceding stem's pitch. It is etymologically related to the low/rising verb solW-~solp- 'report (to a superior)' (p. 208–209) — note solW- itself appears in the W-stem vocabulary list above.

#### Prefinal: Politeness marker -ngi-

Added to show respect for the *listener*, marking the most formal discourse register (hono.ngita, holi.ngita, etc.) (p. 209). A slightly less formal register used contracted forms like hononingta. The source illustrates the resulting speech-level contrast with two extended bilingual dialogue excerpts from the 1447 Sŏkpo sangjŏl: an informal exchange between social equals (Sudatta and Homi) using -ngita/less deferential imperatives, versus a markedly more formal exchange (Sudatta and Śākyamuni) using the fuller hosywosye-style forms — illustrating how register tracked social distance even in 15th-century prose dialogue (p. 209–210).

#### Prefinal: Tense/aspect system

Five prefinal endings, better understood as aspect than strict tense (p. 210): **processive -no-** (ongoing present action, or timeless general statements; uniquely among endings, its pitch was consistently low after a low stem); **durative/aorist -ni-** (drawn-out or time-unbounded action/state, etymologically modifier -(o/u)n + copula); **past/perfective -ke-, -a/e-** (completed action/change of state — -a/e- apparently favored with transitive verbs, -ke- with intransitives/adjectives/the copula; -ke- was also unaccented like -no-, and surfaced as -Ge- after y/l/the copula, with wo- 'come' taking the unique suppletive form -na- instead); **retrospective/imperfective -te-** (recollection of an incomplete-at-the-time past action; realized as -le- after the copula); and **future/conjectural -li-** (etymologically prospective modifier -(o/u)lq + copula, hence sharing the copula's -te-→-le- and -ke-→-Ge- triggering behavior — giving the contrastive sequences -lile- 'incomplete action, future' vs. -liGe- 'completed act, speculative future') (p. 210–212).

#### Prefinal: Emotives

Exclamatory/poetic-effect morphemes: **-two-/-twos-** (incorporating the volitive, found in -twota, -twoswota, -twoswo.ngita; realized as -lwo-/-lwos- after conjectural -li- or the copula); **-s-** (usually co-occurring with the volitive, seen in -kesta, -nwosta, -tasta, -syasta); and **-kwa-/-kwas-** (p. 212–213).

#### Prefinal: Ordering of prefinal endings

Prefinal endings occurred in a largely fixed sequence: (1) deferential -zoW- < (2) past/retrospective -te-/-ke-/-a,e- < (3) honorific -(o/u)si- < (4) present/aorist -no-/-ni- < (5) volitive -wo/wu- < (6) future -li- < (7) emotive -two(s)- < (8) polite -ngi- (p. 213). Past and present were mutually exclusive slots, as were present and future, but past (retrospective) and future (conjectural) could co-occur (-lile-, -liGe-) as an exception to the base ordering. Reordering of the honorific relative to the past/retrospective (-sike-/-site- instead of the expected -kesi-/-tesi-) is attested even in the fifteenth century and became permanently fixed later, in the Early Modern period (p. 213).

> Illustrative composed sequence from the text: **ho-zoW-osi-ni-ngi-ta** "did [honored action]" = ho- (do) + -zoW- (deferential) + -(o/u)si- (honorific) + -ni- (durative/aorist) + -ngi- (politeness) + -ta (declarative) — stacking deference-to-object, respect-for-subject, aspect, and politeness-to-listener in a single predicate (p. 213).

#### Final: Modifier endings -(o/u)n and -(o/u)lq

-(o/u)n marked present/general-quality readings on adjectives and past/completed readings on verbs, but was fundamentally time-neutral — it combined with aspect morphemes to form -non, -ten, -ken, -lin (p. 213–214). It was also, rarely, usable directly as a nominalizer in the fifteenth century (the noun elGwun 'adult' is cited as a fossil of this; see vocabulary above), a use that had ceased to be productive by the sixteenth century. -(o/u)lq marked future/conjectural modification, could double with prefinal -li- as -lilq for emphasis, and was likewise occasionally used as a fifteenth-century nominalizer — curiously transcribed with final /s/ rather than a glottal stop in that nominalized use, and taking no particles (p. 214–215).

#### Final: Nominalizers -(o/u)m and -ki

-(o/u)m, the ordinary nominalizer, was always directly preceded by the volitive, so the functioning suffix was effectively -wom/-wum (mekwum 'eating', capwom 'grasping'); because it incorporated the volitive, it also gained an /l/ after the copula and combined with the honorific as -syam (p. 215). -ki, the now-dominant Contemporary Korean nominalizer, was "extremely rare" in Middle Korean, attested in only a handful of forms (p. 215).

#### Final: Coordinate conjunctive endings

Three endings linked clauses with an "and" meaning (p. 215–217): **-kwo** 'and then/and also' (modern-equivalent usage; often followed by focus/emphatic particles to form -kwon, -kwok, -kwom); **-(o/u)mye** 'and also' (etymologically nominalizer -(o/u)m + postposition ye, a contracted form of the copula's infinitive iye; combinable with -li-, and extendable with sye 'while' or -ng to emphasize simultaneity, giving -(o/u)myesye/-(o/u)myeng); and the **infinitive -a/e** 'and so, -ing' (the all-purpose linking form conventionally called "infinitive" though its usage doesn't map onto the Western grammatical category — it marks a verb in dependent relationship to a following auxiliary-like verb, e.g. tule ka- 'go into' from tul- 'enter' + -e + ka- 'go'; often co-occurs with the emphatic -k or intensifier -m).

#### Final: Causation conjunctive endings

Five endings shared overlapping causal meanings not yet fully distinguished by scholars (p. 217–220): **-(o/u)ni** 'since' (the most common, still in use today, but with a range of looser linking meanings beyond strict causation — 'and,' 'when,' 'if,' 'but then'); **-(o/u)may** 'because' (= nominalizer -(o/u)m + locative particle ay, literally 'in doing/being...'); **-nol/nul** 'as, since, when' (= modifier -(o/u)n + accusative -ol/ul; almost always fused with perfective -ke- as -kenol/kenul); **-(o/u)l_ssoy** 'as, because' (= prospective modifier -(o/u)lq + postmodifier noun so 'the fact that' + locative oy; declarative-only); and **-kwantoy** 'as, because' (the interrogative-only counterpart to -(o/u)l ssoy, incorporating postmodifier noun to + locative oy).

#### Final: Conditional conjunctive endings

**-(o/u)myen** 'when, if' (the most common, little changed from its modern reflex); **-ton/tun** 'when, if' (restricted to combining with the perfective morphemes -ke-/-a,e-, e.g. hoketun); **-(o/u)ntay** 'when, if' (also found with an affixed topic particle as -ntayn/-nteyn, and fused with perfective/retrospective as -kentayn/-tenteyn); and the rarer **-(o/u)lantoy** 'if it's a matter of...then' (p. 219–220).

#### Final: Concessive conjunctive endings

**-(o/u)na** 'but, although, or' (essentially unchanged from its modern reflex); **-toy** 'although' (always with the volitive; also used idiomatically to introduce reported speech after verbs like 'say/ask'); and four rarer forms — **-(o/u)ntol** (modifier -(o/u)n + postmodifier noun to + accusative), **-keniwa** (perfective -ke- + modifier -(o/u)n + noun i 'one' + comitative wa), **-kenmalon** (perfective -ke- + modifier -(o/u)n + a noun mal of uncertain further analysis), **-(o/u)l spwuntyeng**, and **-(o/u)l syentyeng** (both = prospective modifier -(o/u)lq + a postmodifier noun) (p. 219–220).

#### Final: Purposive ending -(o/u)la

'For the purpose of,' etymologically prospective modifier -(o/u)lq + an obsolete Old-Korean locative particle *a (p. 220).

#### Final: Intentive ending -lye

'With the intention of,' used much as its modern reflex, and always co-occurring with the volitive in Middle Korean (p. 220).

#### Final: Desiderative endings

Five endings expressing want/aspiration, split along a self/other axis (p. 220–221): **-kwocye** 'want to' (the most common, for the speaker's own desired action); **-acye** 'would like to' (a less-used synonym of -kwocye, also for the speaker's own desire); **-kwatye** 'want (others) to' (desire directed at a third party's action); **-kwatoyye** 'want (others) to' (a less common synonym of -kwatye); and **-kuyskwo/-keyskwo** 'wants to' (marks the intended *result* of the main verb's action, functioning much like Contemporary Korean -keykkum; realized as -Geyskwo after a stem-final -l).

#### Final: Projective ending -tolwok

'To the point where, until' — projects the limit of an action/state; the variant -twolwok began appearing already in the fifteenth century and displaced the original -tolwok by the sixteenth (p. 221).

#### Final: Increasing-degree endings

**-tiGwos** 'the more...the more...' (the more commonly attested form in the textual record) and the rarer **-(o/u)l[q]_solwok**, ancestral to Contemporary Korean -(u)lq swulwok (p. 221–222).

#### Final: Sequence/alternation endings

**-(o/u)la** 'and then, whereupon' (also doubled with emphatic -k as -(o/u)lak to show alternating actions: wolak kalak 'coming and going') and **-(o/u)nta_mata** 'as soon as, whereupon' (the direct ancestor of Contemporary Korean -ca_maca; both mata and maca derive from the negative imperative verb mal-) (p. 222).

#### Final: Adverbative endings (cross-reference)

Three adverbative endings existed — -i, -wo/wu, and -key(/koy/kuy) — but the authors note these were already treated earlier in the chapter's discussion of suffixes rather than here (p. 222).

#### Final: Negational endings

Five endings co-occurring with a following negative predicate, most etymologically containing the postmodifier noun to 'the fact that...' attached directly to the stem (p. 222–223): **-ti** (the basic negational complementizer, still productive today); **-tol**; **-(o/u)ntwong** (the one member incorporating the modifier -(o/u)n rather than attaching directly); **-tulan**; and **-tiWi** 'but, yet, even though' (etymologically related to -ti but with an obscure second syllable; variously respelled -tiwuy/-tiwoy/-tiwey after the mid-15th century, all attempts to write the same [tiwi] pronunciation — semantically it affirmed the preceding clause more emphatically before the negative that followed).

#### Finite: Declarative ending -ta/-la

Base declarative -ta was replaced by -la after the prefinal endings -wo/wu-, -kwa-, -te-, -li-, -ni-, and after the copula i- — a natural class, since -li- and -ni- are themselves historically modifier + copula (p. 223).

#### Finite: Imperative endings and the three politeness styles

Three direct imperatives, differentiated by deference level, which also give their names to the three main politeness styles still used today to label Korean speech levels: **-(o/u)sywosye** (highest deference, subordinate-to-superior; → hosywosye-chey); **-assye** (intermediate deference; → hoyassye-chey); and **-(o/u)la** (the basic/plain imperative, often with the infinitive -a/e, → hola-chey) (p. 223–224). Beyond direct commands, Middle Korean also had **request-style imperatives** differentiated the same way: **-kwola** (plain request), **-kwolye** (indirect/deferential request), **-kwo.ngita** (highest-deference request, hosywosye-level); and, for requests to facilitate the speaker's own needs, **-cila** (plain) and **-ci.ngita** (deferential) — the latter pair always co-occurring with a perfective morpheme (p. 224–225).

#### Finite: Propositive endings (‘let’s...’)

**-cye** (plain) and **-sa.ngita** (deferential); plain -cye also sometimes combined with the imperative -la. In the sixteenth century -cye began appearing as -cya, the direct ancestor of Contemporary Korean's propositive -ca (p. 225).

#### Finite: Promissory ending -ma

Always co-occurring with the volitive; marks a speaker's promise/commitment to a future action (p. 225).

#### Finite: Interrogative endings

Two base question endings by question type: **-ka** for yes/no questions, **-kwo** for content (wh-) questions, each attaching as a postposition directly to a nominal predicate or, with verbal/adjectival predicates, combining with -ni- (giving -nye/-nywo) or -li- (giving -lye/-lywo) (p. 225–226). Each had a polite counterpart incorporating -ngi-: **-ngiska**, **-ngiskwo**. In the intermediate "hoyassye" style, the yes/no vs. content-question distinction collapsed onto a single shared ending, **-ska** — evidence, per the authors, that the two question types were already beginning to merge in Late Middle Korean. A separate, peculiar interrogative form was used only when the subject was the second-person pronoun ne 'you': the modifier ending -(o/u)n or -(o/u)lq attached directly to -ta, serving for both yes/no and content second-person questions alike. Finally, a rhetorical-question construction used **-i.ston**, **-i.stonye**, or **-i.stoni.ngiska** (p. 226).

## Korean — Established Vocabulary/Grammar: A History of the Korean Language, Ch. 5 "Late Middle Korean," §5.5 Syntax – §5.6 Vocabulary

**Source:** Ki-Moon Lee & S. Robert Ramsey, *A History of the Korean Language* (Cambridge University
Press, 2011), printed pp. 227–240 (PDF pp. 239–252, offset confirmed: PDF_page = printed_page + 12).
Academic diachronic/historical-linguistics monograph, not a teaching grammar. Covers the tail end of
a final-endings discussion (exclamatory mood, continuing from the preceding section 5.4), then the
full sections **5.5 Syntax** (§5.5.1–5.5.10) and **5.6 Vocabulary** (§5.6.1–5.6.3).

**Coverage note:** Clean, genuine text layer — `pdftotext` rendered Hangul (including archaic jamo
and arae-a ㆍ), Hanja, and Yale-romanization diacritics correctly, with one caveat: a small number of
individual Hangul syllable blocks containing the archaic arae-a vowel or now-obsolete jamo render as
a **blank space** rather than the glyph itself (confirmed on at least 4 Hangul example strings — see
the romanized forms, which are unaffected, for the intended reading in those cases; flagged
individually below with "(Hangul gap)"). No vision-reading was needed; no handwritten marginalia
present. The opening ~36 lines (exclamatory mood endings *-twota*, *-kwona*, *-(o/u)ntye*/
*-(o/u)lssye*) are the tail of a final-endings discussion that began before this page range, under
section 5.4 — included here since they fall inside the assigned page range, but flagged as a
likely-partial continuation in case an adjacent chunk already captured the start of this discussion.
This topic is confirmed non-overlapping with all existing established Korean files (all synchronic
modern-Korean teaching grammars); no redundant-content trimming was needed. §5.6 Vocabulary is
genuinely vocabulary-rich (lexical replacement, phonetic symbolism, honorific lexicon) and is
extracted comprehensively per the coverage rule; the many grammatical particles/endings discussed in
§5.5 Syntax are treated as Grammar points rather than Vocabulary-table rows, consistent with how this
project treats bound grammatical morphemes elsewhere. Per dispatch instruction, **Attested Era** is
filled uniformly as "Late Middle Korean (15th–16th c.)" for every entry; the book does not mark
regional/dialectal variants in this range (the strata are chronological — 15th c. vs. 16th c. textual
attestation — not geographic), so **Attested Region**/**Geographic Scope** are "—" throughout.

### Grammar points

#### Exclamatory mood endings (continuation of final-endings discussion, §5.4)

Four exclamatory sentence-final endings are discussed, likely a continuation of a final-endings
section that began on an earlier page outside this chunk's range (flagged in the coverage note
above):

- **-twota**: the most common 15th-century exclamatory ending, composed of emotive *-two-* plus the
  declarative *-ta*. Example: *i kaksi_za nay etninwon mozo.m_ay mastwota* 'precisely this bride is
  the one I wish to have!' (1447 Sŏkpo sangjŏl 6:14ab). p. 227.
- **-kwona**: replaced *-twota* in the sixteenth century; ancestor of Contemporary Korean *-kwuna*.
  Earliest attestation c. 1517 (Pŏnyŏk Pak T'ongsa). p. 227.
- **-(o/u)ntye and -(o/u)lssye**: analyzed etymologically as modifier endings *-(o/u)n*/*-(o/u)lq*
  plus a postmodifier noun (*to* or *so*) plus the copula. Beginning with the 1465 Wŏn'gak kyŏng
  ŏnhae, *-(o/u)lssye* was consistently spelled *-(o/u)lsye* (losing the written reinforcement of
  *s*), paralleling the broader disappearance of initial geminates from the textual record (cross-
  reference to §5.2.1.3 of the book, outside this chunk). pp. 227-228.

#### 5.5 Syntax — overview

The authors frame Middle Korean's overall syntax (verb-final, modifier-before-modified, frequent
ellipsis over pronominalization, a mix of case particles and unmarked "absolute position" phrases) as
essentially continuous with the modern language — but identify specific structural details that have
since changed, covered in §5.5.1–§5.5.10. p. 227-228.

#### 5.5.1 Dependent clauses — genitive-marked subjects

In Contemporary Korean, a dependent (relative/modifying) clause's subject takes the same nominative
marking it would have as an independent sentence. In Middle Korean, by contrast, the subject of a
modifying clause (and of nominalizations) was regularly marked with the **genitive** particle (*oy*/
*uy*) instead — e.g. *SYWU-TTALQ_oy san ke.s* literally "Sudatta's bought thing" for "the thing
Sudatta bought" (1447 Sŏkpo sangjŏl 6:39-40). A secondary feature: personal pronouns were *doubly*
marked as genitives — *nay* 'my' and *ney* 'your' appear as *nay_oy* and *ney_uy* in these
constructions. A further, rarer pattern marks such dependent-clause subjects with the **accusative**
particle *(o/u)l* instead, as if they were direct objects — e.g. *salo.m_oy i.l_ol ... sulnwoni* 'he
is grieved that the affairs of men...' (1481 Tusi ŏnhae 7:16a). pp. 228-229.

#### 5.5.2 Zero case marking

Omission of nominative and accusative particles, while still common in colloquial Contemporary
Korean, was **even more frequent** in the fifteenth century — particularly for unmarked subjects
before adjectives or intransitive verbs, and (even more commonly) for unmarked accusative objects.
Example of unmarked subject: *kwoc tywokhwo yelum hanoni* 'its flowers are good and the fruit is
bountiful' (1447 Yongbi ŏch'ŏn ka, stanza 2). p. 229.

#### 5.5.3 Comitative particles — multiple-marking norm

In Contemporary Korean, when two nouns are coordinated with the comitative particle *wa/kwa*,
normally only the first noun takes it, and case marking for the whole phrase attaches after the
second. In the fifteenth century, the **norm was the reverse-order pattern seen with repeated
marking**: the comitative particle was added after *every* coordinated noun, and the phrase's own
case marking was then added only after the *last* occurrence of the particle — e.g. *CHI-TTWUW_wa
CYENG-CHI_way* "[dental spirants] and ['upright' dentals, marked nominative]" (Hunmin chŏngŭm ŏnhae
14b), with longer chains (up to 3-4 coordinated nouns) attested. Beginning occasionally in the 15th
century and increasingly through the early 16th, the final particle occurrence could be dropped. A
direct philological comparison of the 1518 Pŏnyŏk sohak against its 1588 revision, the Sohak ŏnhae,
shows the older multiple-particle pattern being systematically corrected to the single-final-particle
pattern — evidence the authors use to date this syntactic change to the end of the Middle Korean
period. pp. 229-230.

#### 5.5.4 Other changes in particle use

Three adjectives show complement-marking patterns different from Contemporary Korean:
- ***kot_ho-* 'be alike'** and ***sso-* 'be worth (something)'** originally took complements marked
  with the **nominative** particle *i* (not the comitative, as a modern speaker might expect from the
  English gloss "like X"). *kot_ho-* already began shifting to comitative *wa/kwa* marking within the
  fifteenth century itself, and that pattern became dominant. *sso-* often took **unmarked**
  complements instead.
- ***talo-* 'be different'** took complements marked with the **locative** particles *ay*/*aysye*/
  *oykey*, or with *twukwo* 'than' — e.g. *nalas malsso.m_i TYWUNG-KWUYK_ey talGa* "[our] country's
  language is different from China's" (Hunmin chŏngŭm ŏnhae 1a-b). The comitative *wa/kwa* marking
  modern speakers would expect appears only later, in the Early Modern period, though isolated
  instances occur already in 1447 (Yongbi ŏch'ŏn ka, stanza 51).
pp. 230-231.

#### 5.5.5 Nominalizer -ti

The ending *-ti* (ancestral to modern *-ci*) functioned as a conjunctive ending paired with the
negative auxiliaries *ani* 'not' / *mwot* 'cannot'. It was also used as a **nominalizer** for
complements governed by negative-content adjectives: *elyeW-* 'be difficult' (regularly), and
occasionally *sul_ho-* 'hate to do' and *pwuskuli-* 'be ashamed to do'; one 16th-century citation even
extends it to the positive-content *tywoh-* 'be good'. In all these nominalizing uses, *-ti* was later
replaced by *-ki* — the replacement with *elyeW-* is attested from the early 17th century. pp.
231-232.

#### 5.5.6 Postmodifiers (ssi/ssol/ssoy and ti/tol/tolwo/toy)

Two families of bound postmodifier nouns occurred exclusively after verbal modifier endings and
served purely syntactic roles:
- **ssi, ssol, ssoy** (also spelled si/sol/soy) followed the prospective modifier *-(o/u)lq* and were
  morphemically the postmodifier noun *so* ('the fact/one that...') plus a case particle: *ssi*
  (nominative, chiefly in definitions: *X_’l ssi Y_’Gwo* "having X is [called] Y"), *ssol* (used
  idiomatically with *nilo-* 'tell'), *ssoy* (combined with *-(o/u)lq* to mark causation, "because").
- **ti, tol, tolwo, toy** followed either *-(o/u)n* or *-(o/u)lq* and were built from the postmodifier
  noun *to* ('the fact/one that...') plus a case particle (note: the noun's vowel /o/ elides before
  nominative *i*): *ti* (cognate with conjunctive *-ti*, but paired with the **negative copula**
  rather than *ani*/*mwot*), *tol* (cognate with concessive *-(o/u)ntol* 'though,' here overtly
  accusative), *tolwo* (instrumental — "for X reason"), *toy* (locative — "in doing X").
pp. 232-233.

#### 5.5.7 Nominalization

The overwhelming majority of 15th-century nominalizations used the substantive ending *-(o/u)m*; the
now-dominant modern nominalizer *-ki* was still rare. Traces of an older pattern survive in which the
**modifier endings** *-(o/u)n* and *-(o/u)lq* themselves functioned as nominalizers — e.g. the frozen
noun *elGwun* 'adult' derived with *-(wo/wu)n* from a verb *elu-* 'to marry'; a personal name glossed
*cyekun* 'Kim Little One' from *cyek-* 'little' + *-(o/u)n*. This nominalizing use of *-(o/u)n/-(o/u)lq*
was mostly confined to specific environments by the 15th century: *-(o/u)lq* especially before
negatives (*eps-* 'not exist', negative copula *ani-*) — note that in this nominalizer function, the
final consonant of *-(o/u)lq* was written as *s* (ㅅ) rather than *q* (ᅙ); and *-(o/u)n* with
inflectional endings/postpositions attached directly. The authors note such constructions were likely
more common in Old Korean and already receding by the 15th century. pp. 233-234.

#### 5.5.8 Nominal predicates without a copula

A salient Middle Korean trait: nouns and nominalized predicates could take inflectional endings
**directly**, with no copula. Most strikingly in questions, where interrogative endings attach
straight to nouns — e.g. *i esten [KWANG-MYENG] kwo* 'what kind of light is it?' (1459 Wŏrin sŏkpo
10:7b). Less commonly, nominalized predicates attached directly to the declarative ending *-ta* — e.g.
*wunon_ta* '(does) weep', where *wunon* is itself a nominalization. By the Middle Korean period most
*-ta* occurrences were already with ordinary verbals/adjectivals; these nominal-predicate
constructions are described as surviving traces of an earlier pattern. pp. 234.

#### 5.5.9 Chinese syntactic influences (calques)

Classical Chinese grammatical elements were loan-translated into native Korean function words used
in syntactic calques: 與 'with' → *tamos* 'in addition'; 以 'by, with' → *pse* 'using'; 使 'causing' →
*hoyye*/*hoyyekwom* 'letting, making, forcing'; 及 'and, or' → *mis* 'reaching'. Example: *ne_wa tamos
twu nulku.n_i towoyyasimyen* 'together with you, it becomes two old people' (1481 Tusi ŏnhae 9:16),
a direct translation of Chinese 與子成二老. p. 235.

#### 5.5.10 Stylistic linking

The most striking stylistic trait of Middle Korean writing, per the authors, is the near-total
absence of simple, independent sentences — texts favor a convoluted chaining of clauses into long,
unbroken strings of linked events/thoughts per paragraph, a level of syntactic complexity not found
in Korean writing today. p. 235.

#### 5.6.1 Replacement and innovation — overview

By the 15th century, Korean already had a deep stock of Chinese-derived vocabulary; some of it was
naturalized to the point that its Sinitic origin was *forgotten* and the words were written in Hangul
rather than Hanja (e.g. *syangnyey*, *chapan* — see Vocabulary table). Sound and meaning also drifted
over time (*kannan* → *kanan*; *pwunpyel* 'distinction' → 'worry'), and register splits developed
between literary and colloquial senses of the same Sino-Korean word (*cywungsoyng*). A broader
religious/philosophical substrate shift — Buddhism's dominance (from the Three Kingdoms period
through Koryŏ) giving way to neo-Confucianism by the late Koryŏ and Chosŏn — is cited as a driver of
vocabulary change across this period. The authors trace specific lexical-displacement cases (native
words for 'umbrella,' 'hundred,' 'thousand' disappearing in favor of Sino-Korean equivalents) and use
a direct textual comparison of the 1518 Pŏnyŏk sohak against its 1588 revision, the Sohak ŏnhae, to
pin down numerous specific 16th-century lexical replacements (see Vocabulary table for the full word
list from both strata). pp. 235-238.

#### 5.6.2 Phonetic symbolism — overview

Middle Korean used systematic **vowel-harmony-based phonetic symbolism**: yin/yang ("female/male")
vowel-grade pairs within the same root created subtle connotational contrasts in word meaning —
dark/bright vowel variants of the same word signaling differences of scale, intensity, or quality
(e.g. *pholo-* 'brighter blue/green' vs. *phulu-* 'darker blue/green'; a three-way *hyak-*/*hyek-*/
*hywok-* size-gradation set that Contemporary Korean no longer distinguishes). The authors note two
further consequences: (1) such paired forms sometimes **drifted semantically apart** into
etymologically-related but now-distinct lexical items (*nam-*/*nem-* both originally meaning 'exceed',
already splitting into 'remain' vs. 'cross over' by the 15th century; *sal*/*sel* both counting age
until the Early Modern period, when *sal* specialized to age and *sel* to 'new year'); (2)
**consonant** gradation was used in parallel, with orthographic *s*-clusters serving as an
intensifier where modern Korean uses reinforcement (tensification) — e.g. intensive *stwutuli-*
'thrash' vs. plain *twutuli-* 'beat'; intensive *skuzu-* vs. plain *kuzu-* 'pull, drag', where the
intensive form eventually fully displaced the plain one. pp. 238-240.

#### 5.6.3 Special polite vocabulary — overview

Middle Korean's honorific system included dedicated **lexical** (suppletive) pairs alongside its
honorific affixes, e.g. plain *isi-* 'exist' vs. honorific *kyesi-*; plain *mek-* 'eat' vs. honorific
*cwasi-*/*casi-* — distinctions preserved into the present. But some modern honorific-lexicon items
are explicitly attested as **absent** from Middle Korean: the modern suppletive honorific verb for
'sleep' (*cwumusi-*) did not exist — *ca-* 'sleep' was instead made polite by directly suffixing
honorific *-si-*; the modern humble 1st-person pronoun *ce* did not exist — speakers used the plain
pronoun *na* even before superiors; the modern honorific noun for 'rice/meal' (*cinci*) is not
attested until the late 16th century. A distinct humble-verb strategy is also described: deferential
suffixes (*-zoW-*/*-coW-*) could combine with a passive form of a plain verb to produce a humble
equivalent (e.g. *pwo-* 'see' → passive *pwoy-* → *pwoyzoW-* '(humbly) see'; *pat-* 'receive' →
*patcoW-*). The book also explicitly notes that *malssom* 'speech, language' was **not yet** a
humble/polite-register word distinct from plain *mal* in this period — that distinction is a later
development. Finally, a dedicated polite third-person/reflexive pronoun *cokya* (from Chinese 自家)
existed already in this period, principally used reflexively. pp. 240.

## Korean — Established Vocabulary/Grammar: Chapter 6, "Early Modern Korean" (6.1 Sources, 6.2 Writing and Orthography, 6.3 Phonology, 6.4 Grammar, 6.5 Vocabulary)

**Source:** Ki-Moon Lee & S. Robert Ramsey, *A History of the Korean Language* (Cambridge University Press, 2011), printed pp. 241–286 (PDF pp. 253–298).

**Coverage note:** Clean text layer confirmed via `pdftotext -f 253 -l 298`; no vision-reading needed. A
handful of dense multi-syllable Hangul headwords (especially in 6.2–6.4's example lists) lost an internal
syllable block in extraction, and archaic jamo (arae-a ㆍ, ㅿ, ㅸ, and the raised-dot tone/pitch-accent
diacritics) have no glyph in the embedded font and render blank/empty — these gaps are reproduced as
extracted (do not guess-fill) and flagged "Hangul gap" where they occur; the Yale-romanized form is used
as the primary citation form throughout since it survived intact in every case. This is a diachronic
historical-linguistics monograph, not a synchronic teaching grammar — confirmed non-overlapping with all
46 prior established Korean files (all modern-Korean pedagogical sources). Per the coverage rule, every
named sound change, every grammar-point subsection (6.2–6.4), and every distinct cited vocabulary/loanword
item (6.5, plus the clearest lexical items from 6.1/6.2/6.3's illustrative examples) is captured;
individual spelling-variant tokens used purely to illustrate a single phonological rule (e.g. the dozen
near-identical cluster-confusion spellings in 6.2.1) are folded into that grammar point's prose rather than
each given a separate vocabulary row, to avoid table bloat with non-distinct items. Section 6.1 (Sources)
is bibliographic/philological rather than grammatical; it is summarized briefly under its own heading
rather than forced into "Grammar points" format, since it is not a grammar point but is genuinely part of
this chunk's assigned content.

### Grammar points

#### 6.1 Sources (summary, not a grammar point)

This section is bibliographic rather than grammatical: it surveys the Early Modern period's source
corpus — medical treatises (Tongŭi pogam, 1610), reissued Middle Korean classics (Yongbi ŏch'ŏn ka
re-editions of 1612/1659/1765, whose emendations are themselves linguistic evidence of change), the
Office of Interpreters' language textbooks for Chinese, Japanese (Ch'ŏphae sinŏ, 1676), Manchu, and
Mongolian, royal edicts (yunŭm), vernacular literature (sijo, kasa, the vernacular novel — Hong
Kiltong chŏn, Ch'unhyang chŏn, etc.), diaries/memoirs (Hanjungnok), personal letters, and reference
works on rhyme dictionaries, character readings, and idu writing (pp. 242–252). The methodological
point most relevant to this project: **reissues/re-editions of older texts are valuable precisely
where the editors "corrected" forms they no longer understood** — each correction is direct evidence
that the original form had become obsolete or opaque by the time of the reprint (worked examples under
Vocabulary above: iptesini→eptesini, hyekun→cyekun, woypho-→saki-).

#### Loss of orthographic tone marks, ㆁ, and ㅿ (p. 253)

After the Imjin Wars (1592–98), three systematic orthographic losses mark the transition into Early
Modern Korean: (1) tone-marking diacritic dots disappeared entirely from the orthography by the early
17th century (they had already been neglected in some late-16th-century texts); (2) the letter ㆁ
(used for syllable-final /ng/) fell into disuse and merged graphically with the zero symbol ○ — exactly
as in Contemporary Korean, where the same letter shape is read as zero syllable-initially and /ng/
syllable-finally; (3) the letter ㅿ (/z/) disappeared, surviving only in reprints patterned on Middle
Korean originals. Combined with the earlier loss of ㆆ (glottal stop) and ㅸ (bilabial fricative), this
left a 25-letter functional inventory for writing Korean.

#### Initial consonant-cluster confusion and the reemergence of geminates (6.2.1, pp. 254–255)

Middle Korean had three series of initial clusters (sk-/st-/ss-/sp-; pt-/ps-/pth-; psk-/pst-). In the
17th century the orthographic distinctions among these broke down randomly: psk- alternated with a new
cluster pk- or with sk-; pt- substituted for pst-; pt-/st- and ps-/ss- were mutually confused. By the
18th century the choice was essentially arbitrary; by the 19th century usage settled mostly on
s-clusters (sk-, st-, sp-, sc-), with ps- surviving only as an alternate for /ss/. Independently,
geminate spellings (pp-, tt-, kk-, cc-) reemerged as yet another way to write the same reinforced
consonants. A rare 17th-century sh- (ᄻ) spelling for the verb 'pull' (hhye- > hye- > shye-) is
philologically important: it shows the verb's old reinforced pronunciation had persisted all along,
merely hidden by 16th-century spelling convention that wrote it with a plain consonant.

#### Terminal -s/-t confusion (6.2.2, p. 255)

Final /-s/ and /-t/, cleanly distinguished in 15th-century spelling, became a random orthographic
choice by the 17th century (e.g., mas/mat 'taste', mwos/mwot 'pond'). By the 18th century, -t was
effectively eliminated as a word-final spelling in favor of -s, producing at least one clear
overcorrection (mite 'believing' spelled mise, even though the /t/ there was phonetically [d] before a
following vowel).

#### Medial spelling irregularities: -ll-/-ln- and redundant consonants (6.2.3, pp. 255–256)

Medial -ll- and -ln- became freely interchangeable (pulle/pulne 'calling'), where Middle Korean had
used only -ll-. Separately, scribes sometimes inserted a redundant extra consonant when transcribing a
reinforced or aspirated medial consonant before a following particle (e.g., mwulup.ph_i 'knee+i' instead
of simple mwulphi). The author reads this as evidence that scribes still held the old Middle Korean
spelling norms as an ideal they were aware of but could no longer reliably apply — and, incidentally, as
evidence that a noun + following particle was treated as a single orthographic/psychological word unit.

#### Loss of the voiced fricative series (6.3.1, p. 256)

The 17th-century consonant inventory lacked /W, z, G/ (voiced bilabial, alveolar, and velar fricatives):
W and z had already disappeared within the Middle Korean period, and G disappeared soon after. At the
same time, the reinforced-consonant series became fully developed, now including the affricate /cc/, for
a complete reinforced set /pp, tt, kk, cc, ss, hh/.

#### Loss of G and the split outcome in nouns vs. verbs (6.3.1.1, pp. 256–257)

Following /l/, the voiced velar fricative G was first replaced by -ll- (or variably -ln-) in both verb
inflections and nouns through the 17th century, but then, in nouns specifically, G sometimes simply
dropped leaving a bare -l-. The outcome diverged by word class: verb forms with -lG- consistently became
-ll- (wolGa 'goes up' > wolla, still the modern form), while nouns went through -ll- ~ -l- variation
before settling, by the 18th century, on simple -l- (nwolGay 'song' > nwollay ~ nwolay > modern nolay).
This is a clean case study in how the same input cluster can resolve differently depending on
morphological category.

#### Completion of initial-cluster reinforcement and the new sc- spelling (6.3.1.2, pp. 257–258)

The shift of Middle Korean initial clusters into unitary reinforced consonants (begun in Middle Korean)
is judged to have reached completion by around the middle of the 17th century, based on the cluster
confusion documented above plus the emergence of a wholly new spelling sc- (ㅾ) that, read literally,
would represent a cluster never attested in Middle Korean — it is instead best read as evidence that the
MK cluster pc- had already become the simple reinforced consonant cc-. Direct spelling of cc- (ㅉ) itself
is attested as early as the 1676 Ch'ŏphae sinŏ.

#### Sporadic reinforcement and aspiration, including two Sino-Korean morphemes (6.3.1.3, pp. 258–259)

Beyond cluster-driven reinforcement, plain obstruents reinforced or aspirated sporadically throughout
the Early Modern period for two distinct reasons: (1) sound-symbolic emphasis (unconditioned,
impressionistic reinforcement — e.g., ssut- 'wipe away' < sus-), and (2) regressive assimilation (e.g.,
koskos_ho- 'is clean' > modern kkaykkus_ha-). Strikingly, two individual Sino-Korean morphemes — 雙
'double' (MK swang) and 喫 'eat' (MK kik) — independently acquired reinforced initial pronunciations
(ssang, kkik) by Early Modern times; the 1824 Ŏnmunji explicitly calls these out as the only two "fully
muddy" (全濁) Sino-Korean readings still current in colloquial speech. The character 氏 also
intermittently reinforced to ssi (as in a wife's surname-title), though inconsistently — Yu Hŭi reports
it was still sometimes read si as late as the early 19th century. This is a rare case the book documents
of individual lexical/morphemic reinforcement outside any general phonological rule.

#### Palatalization (6.3.1.4, pp. 258–261) — the chapter's single most significant phonological change

Dental stops /t, th, tt/ became the spirants /c, ch, cc/ before /i, y/; in southern dialects (and
spreading north from there) the velars /k, kh, kk/ underwent the same shift, but in Seoul only the dental
series palatalized, and only quite late. The P'yŏngan dialects (northwest) remain unaffected by the
process to this day — a genuinely durable regional isogloss. Yu Hŭi's 1824 Ŏnmunji is quoted directly
describing both the sound-symbolic rationale speakers gave for the change ("the relative difficulty or
ease of pressing down the chin") and an anecdote dating the change's completion within living memory of
his teacher's family (suggesting the change took hold sometime in the latter half of the 17th or in the
18th century). Textual attestation is clearest in the early-18th-century Waeŏ yuhae (e.g., ti- 'pierce' >
ci-) and confirmed complete by the 1748 Tongmun yuhae. A secondary wave at the start of the 19th century
converted /tuy, thuy, ttuy/ to /ti, thi, tti/ (re-populating the environments the first wave had emptied
out). The author stresses palatalization is broader than the phonemic merger alone: it required /c, ch/
to first become genuinely palatal (from earlier dental affricates), causing /ca, ce, cwo, cwu/ to merge
with /cya, cye, cywo, cywu/ in all positions (clearly attested confused in the 1670 Nogŏltae ŏnhae and
1690 Yŏgŏ yuhae), and it also gave /s/ and /n/ palatal allophones [ʃ] and [ɲ] before /i, y/ — a
subphonemic effect still presumably true of modern Korean.

#### Dropping of initial n- before i, y (6.3.1.5, p. 261)

Word-initial n- before /i, y/ was lost in the latter half of the 18th century; attested first in the 1772
Sipku saryak ŏnhae (nimkum 'king' > imkum) and in royal edicts of 1782–83, becoming the regular pattern
by the 19th century. The author flags this as closely related to palatalization (both concern the
environment before /i, y/) without claiming it is mechanically the same change.

#### Minor consonant changes: l-drop before ph, nasal epenthesis, and the kiwa/kimchi hypercorrection (6.3.1.6, pp. 261–263)

Three smaller consonant phenomena: (1) /l/ dropped before labial /ph/ (alph 'front' > aph); (2) a nasal
unexpectedly developed before an intervocalic affricate in a handful of words (kochwo- 'hide' >
komchwo-, already attested 16th c.; teti- 'throw' > teci- (palatalization) > later tenci- with inserted
/n/); (3) most notably, a hypercorrection pattern in southern (Kyŏngsang/Chŏlla) dialects, where
palatalization/affrication had spread further than in Seoul and additionally turned /ki/ into /ci/ (e.g.
kil 'road' > cil). Because southern speech carried lower prestige, speakers attempting to "correct" their
speech toward the Seoul norm sometimes overcorrected genuinely original /ci-/ words back to /ki-/,
producing today's standard forms kiwa 'tile' (for earlier/more conservative ciwa) and kimchi (for earlier
cimchuy). This is presented as a textbook case of hypercorrection permanently altering the standard
language — not a one-off error that was caught and fixed, but one that won out.

#### Loss of the vowel /o/ (arae-a, ᆍ) (6.3.2.1, pp. 262–264)

The single greatest Early Modern vowel change: loss of the Middle Korean vowel /o/ (ㆍ), in two stages.
Stage one (already underway in the 16th century) was a merger with /u/ in non-initial syllables only.
Stage two — loss in initial syllables as well, completing around the mid-18th century — is best evidenced
in the 1776 Hanch'ŏng mun'gam, where spellings are thoroughly confused between /o/ and /a//u/ (e.g.
laynyen/loynyen 'tomorrow'), and is corroborated by direct contemporary scholarly commentary: Sin Kyŏngjun
(1750 Hunmin chŏngŭm unhae) and Yu Hŭi (1824 Ŏnmunji) both comment explicitly on confusion of ㆍ with ㅏ
and ㅡ in "unrefined" speech. The phoneme was functionally gone from speech by the end of the 18th century,
even though the letter ᆍ itself continued to be used orthographically until the 1933 modern-orthography
reform — an important methodological reminder that a letter's orthographic survival can outlast the
phoneme it once represented by well over a century.

#### Monophthongization of ay and ey (6.3.2.2, pp. 263–264)

After /o/ was lost, the first-syllable diphthong oy merged into ay; subsequently the diphthongs ay [ay]
and ey [əy] monophthongized into simple [ε] and [e] respectively. Evidence comes from "umlaut" spellings
in the 1855 Kwansŏng chegun myŏngsŏng kyŏng ŏnhae, where a following /i/ fronts a preceding [a]→[ε] or
[ə]→[e] (e.g. aski- > oyk.ki- 'sparing of') — a fronting process that could only operate on simple vowels,
not diphthongs, dating the monophthongization to the very end of the 18th century. The diphthongs woy and
wuy had not yet reached their modern values ([wε]/[ö] and [wi]/[ü]) by this period.

#### The resulting 8-vowel system (6.3.2.3, p. 264)

After the loss of /o/ and the monophthongization just described, by the early 19th century Early Modern
Korean had an 8-monophthong system: /i, u(ɨ), wu(u), ey(e), e(ə), wo(o), ay(ε), a(a)/ — structurally
identical in inventory size to the modern standard system.

#### Erosion of vowel harmony (6.3.2.4, pp. 264–265)

Vowel harmony eroded in two separable steps tied to the changes above. First, the 16th-century merger of
/o/ with /u/ in non-initial syllables neutralized a harmony contrast there, making /u/ partly a neutral
vowel. Second, the 18th-century merger of initial-syllable /o/ with /a/ did not itself neutralize a
harmony contrast (both were yang/"light" vowels), but it restructured the system: /a/ inherited /o/'s old
pairing with /u/ in addition to its own existing pairing with /e/. Additionally, /wo/ increasingly shifted
to /wu/ in non-initial syllables, further eroding the stem-ending harmony concord. The overall trajectory
is framed as a steady structural simplification rather than a single abrupt collapse.

#### Minor vowel changes: wuy>uy>i reduction, labialization, vowel fronting (6.3.2.5, pp. 265–266)

Three smaller, lower-impact vowel changes are documented: (1) the diphthong wuy reduced to uy in the 17th
century, and uy was later further reduced to i (muyp- 'is hateful' > mip-), timing not firmly established;
(2) labialization: /u/ labialized to /wu/ after a labial consonant (m, p, ph, pp), collapsing a Middle
Korean contrast (mul 'water' vs. mwul 'group') — attested increasingly from the 1690 Yŏgŏ yuhae and the
rule by the 1748 Tongmun yuhae, dated to around the end of the 17th century; (3) vowel fronting: /u/ > /i/
following a spirant (s, c, ch) in 19th-century texts, though this did not generalize to the Seoul standard
except in the single exception achom > achum > achim 'morning' (see Vocabulary table).

#### Loss of tone/pitch accent and compensatory vowel length (6.3.3, p. 266)

As already established for the 16th century (Chapter 5), Early Modern Korean had completely lost
distinctive pitch/tone by this period — it was not a tone or pitch-accent language. However, syllables
that had historically carried rising tone had also always been bimoraic, and that durational property
outlived the pitch contrast itself, becoming a distinctive vowel-length contrast once pitch was gone.
This length distinction went unmarked orthographically in Middle Korean, but its trace resurfaces in
some 19th-century texts for the vowel e specifically: a historically long ē had a phonetic value close to
/u/ (visible in spellings like skulici < :리- 'avoid').

#### Morphology: loss of free-standing verb stems, and numeral changes (6.4.1, p. 267)

Middle Korean allowed bare verb stems to stand alone (used adverbially, or combined directly into
compounds); this was no longer a live process in Early Modern Korean — surviving instances in texts are
fossilized Middle Korean relics, not new formations (illustrated by the Yongbi ŏch'ŏn ka's kot 'like, as',
which the 1657 reprint reanalyzes by absorbing a previously-separate verb into a new verb ending).
Cardinal numerals changed little, though the higher Middle Korean native numerals won 'hundred' and
cumun 'thousand' were fully replaced by Sino-Korean poyk/chyen. A new, now-uniform ordinal suffix -cay
emerged (ches-cay 'first', twul-cay 'second'), replacing an earlier, less systematic ordinal strategy
(ches-kes 'first' in 1748).

#### Nominal suffixes: collapse of the derivation/nominalization distinction in -(o/u)m; loss of -i; plural -tulh/-ney (6.4.1.1, pp. 267–268)

Middle Korean had kept a formal distinction between -(o/u)m deriving a plain noun directly from a verb
stem, and the same suffix nominalizing a whole sentence (where it required an intervening volitive
-wo/wu-). Early Modern Korean lost this distinction — sentential nominalizations like wulwum 'crying'
lost their volitive and became indistinguishable from the plain derived noun wulum. (The verb chwum
chwuta 'dance a dance' is a partial exception, where it was the verb stem itself, not the nominalization
pattern, that changed from chu- to chwu-.) Separately, the nominalizing suffix -i became wholly
unproductive, surviving only in fossilized forms. The adjective-deriving nominal suffix -(o/u)y (khuy
'size' < khu- 'big') survived into the 18th century but was later reduced phonologically to -i (khi),
which collapsed it with the homophonous derived adverb (khi 'in a big way') — a case where a sound change
destroyed a previously-live morphological contrast. Finally, two plural suffixes persisted from Middle
Korean -tolh and -nay: -tulh (ordinary plural) and -ney (originally a marker of respect, 'and other
esteemed persons'), but -ney lost its honorific value in Early Modern Korean and became plain.

#### Verbal suffixes: causative and passive stem changes (6.4.1.2, p. 269)

Causative stems used -hi- and -wu- (continuing MK -Wo-); the rare causative -o- survived only fossilized
in salo- 'save a life' (itself soon restricted to the compound salocap- 'take prisoner'), while its
sibling ilo- 'erect' disappeared entirely. The MK causative of ho- 'do' (hoy-) became hoi- by the 16th-17th
century, and this hoi- was itself later displaced, in the latter Early Modern period, by a wholly
different causative auxiliary siki- (e.g. il sik.ita 'give work to do'). Separately, one Middle Korean
passive-forming -i- was replaced by -hi- (polWi- 'tread [passive]' > polphi-).

#### Adjectival suffixes: -lwow-/-twoy-, the new -sulew- 'like', and the compound-with-ho- pattern (6.4.1.3, pp. 269–270)

The MK noun-to-adjective suffixes -loW/loWoy- and -toW/toWoy- survived into Early Modern Korean in the
phonologically updated forms -lwow- and -twoy- (phyeylwopti 'bothersome' < SK phyey 'trouble'). A wholly
new suffix -sulew- 'is like, -esque' emerged in the 18th century (wensywusuleun nwom 'a fellow like an
enemy'; chwonsulewon i 'someone like a hick'). Separately, the MK verb-to-adjective suffix -Wo/Wu- became
unproductive, and was replaced by a new, highly productive strategy: combining a verb's adverbial form
with ho- 'do' (kiske_hota 'is happy' < kisk- 'rejoice' + ho-). The author notes this Early Modern
compounding pattern is, today, the single most common way of forming adjectivals from verbs in the
language.

#### Adverbial doublets: mwomzwo/swonzwo (6.4.1.4, p. 270)

The 15th-century adverbs mwomzwo 'personally, by oneself' (< mwom 'body') and swonzwo 'personally, with
one's own hands' (< swon 'hand') diversified in Early Modern texts into four coexisting variants each
(swoncwo/swonswo, mwomcwo/mwomswo), all attested within a single 1617 text — illustrating how thoroughly
unstandardized Early Modern orthography/pronunciation of even common adverbs had become.

#### Nouns and noun phrases: loss of final -h, leveling of stem alternations (6.4.2, pp. 270–271)

Nouns ending in a final -h (a morphophonemic feature of Middle Korean) preserved it into the early Early
Modern period but lost it by the later part (stah 'earth' > stang; cip.wuh 'roof' > cipwung). Separately,
the shape-alternating nouns of the namwo-type ('wood, tree': alternating between namo and nam.k- before
certain particles) were gradually leveled toward a single invariant form, a tendency that had begun
already in the 15th century but was only completed by the end of the Early Modern period, leaving
regional dialects as the last holdouts of the old alternation pattern.

#### Particles: the rise of subject-marker ka (6.4.2.1, pp. 271–272)

One of the chapter's most historically interesting findings: the now-ubiquitous subject-marker suppletive
alternant ka, universally used after vowel-final nouns in Contemporary Korean, is attested only sporadically
in the 16th century and still narrowly environment-restricted in the 17th century — in every 17th-century
textual example the author cites (1676 Ch'ŏphae sinŏ, 1698 Sinjŏn chach'obang ŏnhae), ka attaches only to
nouns ending specifically in -y (poy 'ship', coy 'sediment', thuy 'particles'), never to other vowel-final
nouns. This narrow phonological conditioning is read as direct evidence that the 17th century marks the
very beginning of ka's development, not its establishment — the general spread to all vowel-final nouns
happened later, undocumented in the written record (because ka remained colloquial/non-standard in
writing through the 18th-19th centuries), and the Contemporary Korean usage pattern was first reported
only by foreign missionaries at the very end of the 19th century. This is a clear case of writing lagging
far behind actual spoken-language change.

#### Particles: honorific subject marker, genitive, dative, comitative, vocative, comparative, emphatic (6.4.2.1 cont'd, pp. 272–273)

An honorific subject marker formed by combining the particle sye 'from, at' with the honorific dative
skey (→ skeysye) is identified as the direct ancestor of the modern honorific subject marker; variant
forms kyesye/kyeysye and (in personal letters/memoirs) kyewosye/kyeywosye are traced instead to the
honorific existential verb kyeysi-/kyewosi- rather than to skeysye, despite superficial resemblance — an
explicit warning against assuming formal similarity implies etymological identity. The genitive system
simplified to a single particle uy, with the earlier Middle Korean genitive s ("medial s", 사이시옷)
losing all syntactic productivity and surviving only inside fixed compounds. The dative system simplified
to a two-way split: uykey (plain animates) vs. skey (honorific), displacing the earlier verbal-noun-only
particle key. The comitative particle fixed as wa (post-vowel) / kwa (post-consonant), with an earlier
exception (kwa also after -y) soon regularizing to wa only. The vocative particle ha, an honorific used by
inferiors addressing superiors in Middle Korean, dropped out of use entirely. The comparative postposition
twokwon (MK twukwotwukwon) was gradually displaced across the 18th-19th centuries by a new form pwotaka
(derived from the conjunctive form of pwo- 'see' + the "transferentive" mood marker -taka), which by the
19th century was the sole comparative marker — ancestor of modern pwota. The emphatic/focus markers
simplified to just ya (replacing MK za around the end of the 16th century) and kwos (now restricted mostly
to negative contexts).

#### Pronouns: double subject-marking (nay_ka), the honorific reflexive, interrogative lexicalization (6.4.2.2, pp. 273–274)

Three notable pronoun developments: (1) Contemporary nay_ka 'I (subj.)' and ney_ka 'you (subj.)' are
historically double-marked — the MK nominative forms nay/ney already contained the subject particle -y
(a form of i), and the Early Modern period added the newer suppletive ka on top of that already-marked
form; nay_ka is first attested at the very end of the 18th century in the Korean-Japanese textbook Ringo
taihō. (2) The MK third-person honorific reflexive cokya 'himself/herself' (equivalent to modern caki) is
attested preserved specifically in palace speech as coka (quoted directly from the 1613 court-lady diary
Kyech'uk ilgi, where a princess self-referentially rejects the honorific "Princess Herself" address in
favor of a plain title — a vivid sociolinguistic illustration of register/distance marking inside the
royal household). (3) The MK interrogative nwu 'who' + inflectional -kwo/-kwu (giving nwukwo/nwukwu)
fused into a single lexicalized word in Early Modern Korean, with the consequence that a second,
independent interrogative ending had to be added at the end of the whole sentence when forming a question
— a clear case of a transparent morpheme-combination becoming opaque and requiring new morphology to
restore the original function.

#### Pronouns: interrogative mues/muses > mwues; enu loses pronominal status (6.4.2.2 cont'd, p. 274)

Two competing 17th-century forms of 'what' (mues, muses — already coexisting as far back as the 1588
Sohak ŏnhae) were unified into the single 18th-century form mwues (attested in both Manchu-glossary
sources). Separately, the interrogative enu 'which', which in Middle Korean had full pronoun status
(could take case particles like any other substantive), lost that capacity in Early Modern Korean and
became restricted to its modern function as a prenominal modifier only.

#### Conjugations: loss of the -W-/-z- stem alternations and stem mergers (6.4.3, pp. 274–276)

The loss of the voiced spirants /W/ and /z/ reshaped several Middle Korean inflecting-stem classes. Where
a stem ended in -W-, the consonant simply weakened to semivowel -w-, a relatively mild change. Where a stem
ended in -z- (e.g. ciz- 'make', alternating ciz-/cis- before vowels/obstruents respectively), the
consonant was lost outright, producing a new alternation ci-/cis- in which the vowel-stem form ci- behaves
differently from a true vowel stem by still requiring an epenthetic /u/ before certain endings (ciuni, not
*cini). A rare stem-cluster reduction -sk->-kk- affected mast- 'entrust', which irregularly became math-,
while its derived causative masti- instead palatalized to masci- (and only later, by analogy, to modern
maski-) — an explicit case where two morphologically related forms of the same root diverged onto
different regular sound-change paths. Several whole irregular-conjugation classes merged once G was lost:
talo- 'is different' (alternating talo-/talG-) fell together with l-doubling stems like hulu- 'flow'; more
strikingly, stems that had contained both z and G (pozo- 'break', kuzu- 'drag', pizu- 'decorate', swuzu-
'is boisterous') underwent compound changes (pozo- > pasu-; kuzu-/skuzu- > kuu-/skuu-, which itself later
contracted to ske and was reanalyzed as a prefix) — one of the more structurally disruptive consequences
of the Middle Korean fricative-loss cascade. Separately, nye- 'travel about' (a MK stem with an occasional
variant ney-) regularized fully to ney- by Early Modern times, and the honorific existential kyesi-
likewise became kyeysi- in the same period; the non-honorific existential's three-way MK alternation
(isi-/is-/si-) collapsed to the single form is-.

#### Conjugations: morphophonemic simplifications — loss of k~G alternation, weakening of vowel harmony, t~l alternation (6.4.3 cont'd, p. 276)

Three further morphophonemic simplifications: (1) the MK alternation of /k/ with the voiced velar
fricative /G/ (e.g. mul_Gwa 'water+comitative', alGwo 'know+gerund') disappeared with the loss of /G/,
with /k/ restored almost everywhere except after the copula, where the gerund iGwo 'is, and...' instead
became iywo (not *ikwo) — the single surviving trace of the old alternation. (2) Vowel harmony's grip on
suffix-stem concord weakened to essentially the modern system, where almost the only surviving harmony
alternation is the infinitive -a/e. (3) The MK rule governing conditioned /t/~/l/ alternation (most
visible in the copula's inflection) likewise changed.

#### Prefinal endings: collapse of the three-way honorific system to a two-way one (6.4.3.1, p. 277)

Middle Korean's three independent prefinal honorific/politeness endings — honorific -(o/u)si-,
deferential -zoW-, and politeness -ngi- — reduced in Early Modern Korean to a simpler two-category
system (honorific status vs. politeness only). The mechanism: the deferential marker stopped functioning
independently and was absorbed into the marking of general politeness, so that what had been a single
politeness ending (-ngi-) became a complex blended ending (the Early Modern forms -opnoyng.ita,
-opnoyita, -opnoita, all tracing to MK -zopno.ngita) that fuses the old deferential with the old
politeness marker. This fused ending is identified as the direct etymological ancestor of today's Seoul
standard polite ending -(u)pnita — a clean, explicitly-traced example of three morphological categories
collapsing into two via one marker's absorption into another's domain, surviving in the modern
"deferential-polite" style's name as a historical fossil of the original 3-way distinction.

#### Tense and aspect: the new past -as/es- and future -keyss- (6.4.3.1 cont'd, pp. 277–278)

A genuinely new past-tense marker -as/es- emerges in the period, formed by combining the infinitive -a/e
with the existential verb is-; it is attested clearly used to translate Manchu past tense in the 1704
Samyŏk ch'onghae (mullichyesta 'defeated'). The modern future marker -keyss- must also have developed in
this period on internal-reconstruction grounds (likely < adverbative -key + existential is-), but — like
subject-marker ka — it left almost no trace in the written record until foreign missionaries documented it
at the very end of the 19th century, another case of colloquial innovation running well ahead of the
written standard. Separately, the MK processive -no- + declarative -ta sequence (-nota) restructured into
-nta (after vowel-final stems) / -nunta (after consonant-final stems); -nta is already visible in some
16th-century texts, while -nunta first appears only in the 17th century.

#### Emotives: near-total loss except -two (6.4.3.1 cont'd, p. 278)

Of the Middle Korean emotive prefinal endings, only -two survived productively into Early Modern Korean;
-twos, -s-, -kwa-, and -kwas- all became unproductive.

#### Final endings — modifiers/nominalizers: -non/-ten survive, -lin/-ken lost; -(o/u)m loses the volitive; rise of -ki (6.4.3.2, pp. 277–279)

Of the four MK compound modifier endings built on -(o/u)n + another prefinal ending (-non, -ten, -ken,
-lin), only -non (> -nun) and -ten survived into Early Modern Korean; -ken and -lin dropped from the
record, as did the separate future-marked nominalizer -lilq. The nominalizer -(o/u)m, which in Middle
Korean required the volitive -wo/wu- as an intermediate step, came to be used directly once the volitive
fell out of use generally — completing the same nominalization/derivation collapse already discussed under
nominal suffixes. A further detail: in nominalizations of /l/-final stems, the old epenthetic linking
vowel stopped appearing (kolm 'replacement', not kolum), and this exact pattern survives as a fossil in
modern alm 'knowledge' and salm 'life'. Separately, the nominalizer -ki, rare in Middle Korean, became
extremely common in Early Modern Korean — one of the clearest markers distinguishing Early Modern from
Middle Korean syntax (also discussed under Syntax below).

#### Final endings — conjunctive endings simplify (6.4.3.2 cont'd, pp. 278–279)

Several MK conjunctive-ending-plus-postposition/focus-marker combinations (-kwon, -kwok, -kwom,
-(o/u)myesye, -(o/u)myeng, -ak, -am, all built from -kwo/-(o/u)mye/-a/e) were lost wholesale except
-(o/u)myesye, which itself later developed into the modern -( u)myense 'while...ing.' A parallel ending
-(o/u)myeng (built on the postposition -ng) survived only as a fossilized idiom womyeng kamyeng 'coming
and going.' Among fully lost endings: -tiwos and -tiWi (> -tiwuy). Separate smaller shifts: MK
-wo/wutoy (volitive + concessive -toy) became -twoy (a change already starting in late Middle Korean);
the less-common concessive -kenmalon became -kenmanon (also starting in late Middle Korean); the
desiderative -kwatye became -kwatya (16th c.) then -kwacya (via palatalization, Early Modern); and the
rare -(o/u)l[q] solwok eclipsed and effectively replaced the once-more-common -tiGwos, changing form to
-(u)lq swolok (ancestor of modern -(u)lq swulwok 'the more... the more...').

#### Finite endings: declaratives, imperatives/propositives, interrogatives, exclamations (6.4.3.2 cont'd, pp. 279–281)

Declaratives: a rare new ending -lwa appeared (wo.lwa '[he] came'); the ordinary declarative -ta
underwent systematic shortening in several compound forms (MK -te.ngita/-no.ngita/etc. > EM -tey/-noy/
etc., with -ta itself elided from the compound). Imperatives/propositives: the MK deferential imperative
-(o/u)sywosye was fully replaced by -swo; a new first-person-plural propositive -opsay 'let's...' emerged
(still the polite propositive in some modern dialects), composed of deferential -op- (< -zoW-) + -say (<
MK deferential propositive sa.ngita, with -ta elided) — directly parallel in formation logic to the polite
declarative discussed above; the plain propositive -cye became -cya. Interrogatives: most MK interrogative
endings persisted, but the yes/no vs. question-word question distinction gradually disappeared, and the
compound endings -nye/-lye (built on modifier -n/-lq + interrogative -ye) changed form to -nya/-lya (a
change beginning in the 16th century, firmly established by the 17th); the MK rhetorical-question endings
-i.ston/-i.stonye disappeared entirely. Exclamations: the 16th-century ending -kwona became the dominant
exclamatory marker, joined by new endings -kwoya/-kwoyya; the older ending -twota survived but became less
common, developing an unexplained reinforced variant -stwota after the modifier ending -nun; -(o/u)lssye
became -(o/u)lssya.

#### The postmodifier cay 'while continuing to...' (6.4.3.2 cont'd, p. 279)

A postmodifier cay (< MK cahi) attached to a verb/adjective's modifier form to mark continuity of the
action/state (hwantwo chon cay woloni 'having strapped on his sword, he went up'); the author identifies
it as cognate with the ordinal-forming suffix -cahi/chahi — i.e. the same morpheme doing double duty as
both an ordinal-number formant and a continuity/durative marker.

#### Syntax: frozen postmodifier nouns, the rise of -ki nominalization, and the shift away from nominal-head sentences (6.4.4, pp. 281–282)

Three syntactic-level developments round out the grammar section. (1) The MK postmodifier nouns to and so
(roughly corresponding to modern kes 'the fact/thing that...') stopped being productive, surviving only
in a few lexically frozen constructions. (2) -ki nominalizations, rare in Middle Korean, became one of the
clearest hallmarks of Early Modern syntax, at the expense of -(o/u)m's productivity; relatedly, the MK
endings -(o/u)n and -(o/u)lq, which had occasionally also functioned as nominalizers in Middle Korean,
lost that secondary function entirely and became pure modifier endings, exactly as in the modern language.
(3) Most strikingly, Middle Korean sentences (especially interrogatives) were predominantly nominal-head
sentences at root — a noun-predicate question took an interrogative ending (-ka/-kwo) fused directly onto
the noun itself (nwu + kwo > nwukwu 'who [is it]?'). Early Modern Korean shows a clear structural drift
toward verbal/adjectival/copular-headed sentences instead — the same noun-plus-fused-ending structure
persisted as an option (i pes_un nwukwo_kwo 'who is this friend?', 1670) but was increasingly superseded
by structures where the noun predicate is followed by an explicit copula construction (ney nwu.yn_ta 'who
are you?', 1704, noun + copula inta). This is presented as evidence of a broader typological shift in
sentence-formation strategy across the period, distinct from (though related to) the lexicalization of
nwukwu discussed under Pronouns above. The author also notes that extremely long, multiply-embedded
sentence structures (especially in the vernacular novel genre) persisted throughout the Early Modern
period and were only significantly simplified in the Contemporary Korean era.

#### 6.5 Vocabulary: overview of lexical change (pp. 282–286)

The vocabulary section's overarching finding is that lexical change in this period ran in several
independent directions simultaneously: wholesale loss of native words (crowded out by Sino-Korean
equivalents for everyday referents like 'mountain,' 'river,' 'relatives,' 'gate' — see Vocabulary table);
outright obsolescence of native inflecting words for unclear reasons unrelated to Sinitic competition
(iW-, woy-, woypho-, hyek- — directly evidenced by 17th-century reprint "corrections" of forms editors no
longer understood); semantic drift/narrowing within surviving native words (eyespu-, eli-, cyem-,
solang_ho-, cus, him, pit/ssota/pissata — see Vocabulary table for each); semantic shift specific to
Sino-Korean morphemes used in uniquely Korean ways unattested in Classical Chinese (wencyeng, incyeng,
pangswong, hawok, tungtoy, palmyeng, cyengchyey); fresh borrowing directly from Classical/Mandarin Chinese
(nywu, soyngsim_ina, and the larger list in Isu sinp'yŏn/Aŏn kakpi); indirect Western-origin borrowing
mediated through Chinese or Japanese contact (comyengcywong, chyenlikyeng, tampay); and a smaller stream
of Manchu-origin loanwords, carefully distinguished by the author from the larger set of words once
misattributed to Manchu that were in fact independently borrowed by both Manchu and Korean from Mongolian
(nelkhwu, swopwuli, khulimay, mahulay — see Vocabulary table). The historical anecdote about Jan Janse
Weltevree (1628) and Hendrik Hamel (1653), the first attested Westerners to reach Korea, frames the
chapter's account of how Western contact entered the Korean lexicon only very indirectly (via Peking)
until the late 19th century. The pwoli 'glass' case (see Vocabulary table) is the single richest
loanword-nativization example in the chapter, explicitly tracing phonetic drift away from the Chinese
source alongside a documented pun (moykkyeng 'barley glasses').
