## Korean — Established Vocabulary/Grammar: A History of the Korean Language, Ch. 7 "Contemporary Korean" (full chapter)

**Source:** Ki-Moon Lee & S. Robert Ramsey, *A History of the Korean Language* (Cambridge University
Press, 2011), printed pp. 287–305 (PDF pp. 299–317). Chapter 7, sections 7.1–7.6: "Contemporary
Korean" (script reform, language standardization, phonological/morphological/syntactic/lexical
trends). Academic diachronic monograph — this is its final chapter, covering the late-19th-century
"enlightenment period" through the early 21st century. Clean text layer confirmed via `pdftotext -f
299 -l 317`; no font cipher, no vision-reading needed; Hangul/Hanja/Yale-romanization diacritics all
rendered correctly throughout — no Hangul-gap or missing-glyph issues encountered in this chunk.

**Overlap check against `established/033_lets_learn_korean_northern_dialect.md` (performed before
extraction, per dispatch instructions):** 033 is a 1989 DPRK-published pedagogical primer that
documents the *synchronic phenomenon* of 두음법칙 (initial-sound-law) non-application in North Korean
orthography/pronunciation extremely thoroughly, with dozens of attested example pairs (래일/내일,
녀자/여자, 로동자/노동자, 륙/육, 리발소/이발소, etc.) plus DPRK-specific vocabulary (동무, 주체,
문화어-adjacent ideological terms) and North Korea's distinctive irregular-consonant-letter names.
**This academic chapter does NOT re-explain the 두음법칙 mechanism, origin, or dating anywhere in the
assigned page range** (confirmed by grep — no occurrence of "두음법칙"/"initial sound" in pp. 287–305);
that historical/phonological account, if this book gives one at all, must live in an earlier chapter
(likely the Middle/Early Modern Korean material, outside this chunk's range) — so there is no
redundant 두음법칙 content to skip or deduplicate here, and nothing new on that specific topic to add
either. **What this chapter does cover that 033 never touches at all** (confirmed genuinely
non-redundant, so kept in full): the formal institutional/documentary history of Hangul-orthography
standardization (Chi Sŏgyŏng 1905 → Kungmun Yŏn'guso 1907 → Chu Sigyŏng → 1933 "Proposition for the
Unification of Hangul Orthography" → South Korea's 1988 "Hangul Orthography"); the script-reform
history from four-script Chosŏn-era writing (Hanmun/Hanmun-idu/ŏnmun/ŏnhanmun) through mixed-script
to Hangul-exclusive publishing, including North Korea's *separate and earlier* script-reform history
(Kim Il Sung's complete 1949 ban on Hanja, vs. South Korea's much slower, market-driven phase-out
completing only by the 1990s — explicitly dated and regionally attributed, unlike anything in 033);
the one substantive overlap point — North Korea's "Cultured Language" (문화어) standard — is treated
here with a historical nuance 033 has no occasion to supply (the DPRK's own claim that 문화어 is
Pyongyang-based is only "partially true"; standard Korean in the North was Seoul-based until 1945 and
has "yet to diverge significantly, except in the official vocabulary used by the state"); and the
entirety of sections 7.3–7.6 (phonological trends, morphological leveling, the six-level honorific
speech-style system, syntax, and vocabulary/loanword history), none of which overlaps 033's scope at
all. **Kept: everything in this chapter.** Nothing was skipped as redundant with 033 — the one
topical overlap (문화어) is treated as a distinct, deeper historical claim, not a duplicate.

**Coverage note:** every grammar/historical point and every distinct illustrative lexical example
from this full chapter; repeated restatements of the same point are consolidated. Copyright
discipline observed — paraphrase plus short illustrative quotes only, page numbers cited throughout.

---

### Grammar points

#### Four competing scripts before reform (7.1)

Mid-19th-century Korea used four distinct kinds of writing: Hanmun (Classical Chinese, most
prestigious, used for formal writing by the elite), ŏnmun (Hangul, least prestigious and least
standardized), and two intermediate "middleman" styles used by clerks — idu and ŏnhanmun (a mixed
script of Chinese phrases linked by Hangul particles/endings). The "unification of the written and
spoken language" (Ŏnmun ilch'i) movement of the enlightenment period, named after a parallel Japanese
initiative, generally agreed Classical Chinese and idu were obsolete, but ŏnmun-only vs. mixed-script
advocates remained at odds "for decades (and even today to a certain extent)" (p. 288).

#### Mixed-script to Hangul-exclusive transition (7.1)

Early 20th century: mixed script replaced Classical Chinese for formal writing, led by Yu Kilchun's
1895 travel diary *Sŏyu kyŏnmun*. After the March 1st Movement (1919), Chinese characters came to be
used only within mixed-script Korean-language texts, never standalone. All-Hangul writing had
separately continued uninterrupted in the Early Modern classic-novel tradition, and Sŏ Chaep'il's
*Tongnip sinmun* ("The Independent," 1896) became Korea's first modern, all-Hangul newspaper (p.
288–289).

**North/South divergence in the final transition to Hangul-only script (explicit historical
dating, p. 289):** "In 1949, Kim Il Sung brought about a complete end to the use of Chinese characters
in North Korea, because he associated mixed writing with Japanese practice." **South Korea's parallel
shift was much slower and market-driven**, not a single policy act: mixed writing remained the rule
in SK newspapers and professional books (never in fiction) through the colonial period and beyond,
with Chinese-character frequency only "steadily" declining until "by the 1990s, most major South
Korean dailies had phased out" Hanja. Today Hanja in South Korea survives mainly as newspaper-headline
abbreviations (美 "America," 日 "Japan") and parenthetical Sino-Korean clarifications; a "mild
resurgence" of Hanja-education advocacy began in the early 21st century, but the author judges a
return to mixed-script publishing "remote."

#### Orthographic standardization history (7.1.1)

The movement's "next order of business" after script choice was Hangul spelling itself, which had
been wildly inconsistent through the late Chosŏn period. Formal history, with dates: activist **Chi
Sŏgyŏng**'s 1905 public proposal for "new standards" of spelling/usage attracted enough attention to
affect government policy — the first document to do so. In 1907 the newly founded **Kungmun Yŏn'guso**
(Institute for the Study of Korean Writing) took up the proposal, with scholar **Chu Sigyŏng** (founder
of the Kungmun Tongsikhoe, "Society for the Standardization of Korean Writing") as its most important
contributor. Chu — also credited with coining the term "Hangul" around 1912, and author of *Kugŏ
munpŏp* (1910) and *Mal ŭi sori* (1914) — had already laid out new-orthography principles by the end
of the 19th century. After two more decades of scholarly work, the **Korean Language Society**
(Chosŏnŏ hakhoe, later renamed Han'gŭl hakhoe) issued the landmark **"Proposition for the Unification
of Hangul Orthography" (1933)**, based largely on Chu's principles, which remained the governing
standard "for the next half century." **In 1988, the South Korean Ministry of Education promulgated
"Hangul Orthography,"** which (with minor subsequent tweaks) remains the current rule book (p.
289–290).

#### Principles of contemporary orthography — the morphophonemic basis (7.1.2)

Both the 1933 and 1988 documents preserved the existing alphabet with minimal symbol change: they
dropped the obsolete vowel letter ᆞ (its sound [ʌ] no longer existed in Seoul speech) and switched the
reinforced-consonant transcription from consonant-cluster spellings (ᄭ, ᄯ, ᄲ, ᄡ, ᄶ) to doubled-letter
spellings (ᄁ, ᄄ, ᄈ, ᄊ, ᄍ) — otherwise leaving conventions like the ᅌ "zero-initial"/syllable-final-ng
symbol and the diphthong-style spellings of now-monophthongized vowels (ᅢ, ᅦ, ᅬ) untouched. The core
principle, from Article 1 of the 1933 document: "Write the standard language according to its
sounds, but make it fit the language rules" — in practice, a **morphophonemic** orthography in which
the word's "basic" underlying shape takes precedence over surface pronunciation (e.g. 값 "value" is
always spelled with final -s even though it's unpronounced before a consonant), with irregular
verb/adjective inflections as the only exceptions. The author stresses this is "exactly the opposite"
of 15th-century Hangul orthography, which had been strictly phonemic (p. 290–291; cf. Ch. 5).

#### Language standardization — defining the standard dialect (7.2)

The 1933 orthography Proposition's introduction contained the first explicit statement of a Korean
standard language: "The Standard Language is to be Seoul speech now generally used in middle-class
society." A dedicated committee (est. 1935) produced the **1936 "Collection of Standard Language
Vocabulary"** booklet, listing standard forms for over 6,000 words. **South Korea's 1988 "Standard
Language Rules"** refined the definition to "Contemporary Seoul speech generally used by educated
people." In practice, the standard was built on early-20th-century "new novel" prose, became the
language taught in schools after 1945, and has since spread nationwide via schooling, migration to
Seoul (now home to a quarter of South Korea's population), media, and military service, displacing
regional dialects especially among the young (p. 291–292).

**North Korea's parallel standard — "Cultured Language" (문화어, munhwaŏ) (p. 292):** the DPRK
government "claims" this standard is Pyongyang-based, but the author states this is "only partially
true": Seoul speech was the standard in the North too until 1945, and "what is spoken today in the
north has yet to diverge significantly, except in the official vocabulary used by the state." Regional
dialect leveling is said to be proceeding even faster in the North than in the South.

#### Phonology — consonants (7.3.1.1)

The consonant system has been largely stable since the 19th century; palatalization changes were the
last major restructuring and had already run their course. Stops/affricates keep a three-way
plain/aspirated/reinforced ("glottalized") contrast (p/ph/pp, t/th/tt, k/kh/kk, c/ch/cc); fricatives
have only a two-way plain/reinforced contrast (s/ss), with plain *s* behaving enough like an aspirate
(no intervocalic voicing) that some phonologists reclassify it. Korean lost its one reinforced glottal
fricative, *hh* (which mostly became *kh*) — attested as late as the 17th century in Early Modern
Korean. A long-term historical trend toward "increasing implosiveness" means only 7 consonants (p, t,
k, m, n, ng, l) can close a syllable before a juncture today, all unreleased, with aspiration/
reinforcement/affrication distinctions neutralized word-finally (e.g. p/ph → [p]; t/th, c/ch, s/ss all
→ [t]) — a neutralization the author notes was not complete until "around the turn of the sixteenth
century" for the s/t distinction specifically. No word-initial consonant clusters exist; word-final
clusters are likewise reduced by regular phonological rule (p. 292–294).

#### Phonology — vowels (7.3.1.2)

Korean's two front rounded vowels ([ö], [ü]) are historically recent monophthongizations of -y-glide
diphthongs (woy, wuy) that were apparently still diphthongal in the 19th century per philological
evidence; today they surface as [we]/[wi] word-initially, but as true front-rounded [ö]/[ü] after a
(especially palatal) initial consonant — though **many young Seoul speakers have lost the front
rounded vowels entirely**, using [we]/[wi] everywhere; they remain part of the "ideal" standard system
nonetheless. The vowel traditionally romanized *e* varies allophonically by length — short [ʌ]-like,
long [ə:] — a difference already present in 19th-century transcriptional confusion and still audible
in Sino-Korean doublets. The standard vowel system itself is actively eroding: besides the front
rounded vowels, the historically distinct *ay* [ε] / *ey* [e] have merged for almost all Seoulites
under ~50; the sequence *uy* ᅴ is diphthongizing to [ɨy] in younger speech (a structural anomaly,
since -y offglides otherwise died out after 19th-century monophthongization) even though older Seoul
speakers still treat *uy* as purely morphophonemic ([ɨ] initially, [i] non-initially) — the genitive
particle 의 (historically /uy/) is now always pronounced [e] regardless of age (p. 293–296).

**Vowel harmony's residue (p. 296):** the Middle Korean vowel-harmony system has largely broken down
in regular inflection, but the "bright" (yang: a, wo) vs. "dark" (yin: e, wu) vowel opposition remains
"remarkably productive" in mimetic/onomatopoeic vocabulary (e.g. allaktallak vs. ellek-tellek) and
survives as a conditioning factor on the infinitive ending -a/-e, though -e is increasingly displacing
historically-conditioned -a even after bright-vowel stems in casual Seoul speech.

#### Phonology — suprasegmentals: vowel length as tonal relic (7.3.1.3)

Seoul Korean's distinctive vowel length is a direct historical descendant of the Middle Korean tone
system (tones were lost in the central dialect in the 16th century, leaving the former "rising tone"
as a long vowel), producing word-initial minimal pairs like 말 ma:l "speech" vs. 말 mal "horse." This
length contrast is **not preserved in non-initial position** — e.g. 대 tay: "big" stays long in 대학
tay:hak "college" but shortens in 확대 hwaktay "magnification" — a pattern especially visible in
Sino-Korean compounds, and increasingly lost even in casual speech of originally-initial long vowels
(p. 296–297).

#### Morphology — noun allomorphy leveling (7.4.1)

Middle Korean nouns with non-automatic allomorphic alternation (e.g. namwo~namk- "tree," kwumwu~kwumk-
"hole," nwolo~nwolG- "roe deer," azo~azG- "younger brother") have leveled to single invariant shapes
in the Seoul dialect (namwu, kwumeng, nwolwu, awu), with only a few archaic idioms preserving the old
forms. Final-consonant morphophonemic alternation is likewise being leveled in casual speech (e.g.
꽃/꽃이 kkwoch/kkwoch-i optionally → 꽃/꽃이 with final -s instead of -ch). A historical MK noun-final
*h* (lost before vowel-initial particles) survives only as a trace in the irregular behavior of 하나
hana "one" and in frozen compounds like 암탉/수탉 "hen/rooster" (< amh/swuh "female/male" + talk
"chicken") (p. 297–299).

#### Morphology — particle history: ka, medial-s, and broadened uy (7.4.1.1)

The now-ubiquitous subject particle **ka** is attested only rarely in late Middle Korean texts and
remained textually rare through the Early Modern period (where *i* was the only subject particle
written down), even though late-19th-century foreign-missionary records show *ka* was already common
in actual speech by then, alternating suppletively with *i*. **It only became regular in writing during
the Contemporary period.** The Middle Korean genitive particle **-s-** (사이ᄉ, "medial s") became
unproductive as a genitive marker in the Early Modern period and now survives only compound-internally,
with its exact semantic contribution (e.g. distinguishing 나뭇집 "lumber yard" from 나무집 "wooden
house") still not fully understood even by contemporary scholarship. The genitive particle **uy**,
originally restricted to animate-noun contexts, has broadened to inanimates and now combines with
locative particles (에의, 에서의) in constructions the author attributes to English/Japanese calquing
(p. 299).

#### Morphology — pronoun changes (7.4.1.2)

Post-Early-Modern pronoun changes: subject forms 나/너 → 내가/네가 (ka added to the old nominative
forms, which already fused the pronoun with the older subject particle *i*); loss of the ay/ey
distinction among younger speakers created the newer subject form 니가 and a corresponding genitive
니. The honorific first-person pronoun 저 (ce), with subject form 제가 and genitive 제, is a Contemporary-period
innovation. The interrogative "who" gained a second form 누구 alongside older 누; subject 누가 replaced
누이; genitive 누구의 is now more common than the literary 누의 (p. 299–300).

#### Morphology — copula and negation changes (7.4.2)

The copula's direct-style indicative 이다 ita replaced Middle Korean 이라 ila; the gerund 이고 ikwo is
now near-exclusive in ordinary speech, displacing MK 이요 iywo (which survives in formal/written
registers). Negation history: 아니 ani began as a precopular noun (아니다), separately functioned as an
adverb negating inflecting forms, and in the Early Modern period additionally came to inflect directly
as a verb-negating pattern — but that inflecting-negative pattern was lost by the Contemporary period,
replaced by (하지) 아니하다 / its contraction (하지) 않다 (p. 300).

#### The honorific system — structural reduction (7.4.2.1)

Korean's inflectional honorific system historically had three categories: subject honorification,
**object exaltation**, and speech style. **Object exaltation is "no longer a productive part of the
inflectional system"** — the Middle Korean suffix -sop- that marked it is gone, replaced by
analytic strategies (the particle 께 for indirect objects, special polite verbs like 모시다 "escort").
Subject honorification via -(u)si- remains fully productive. The "style" system — reflecting
speaker/addressee social relationship — has (by some scholars' count) six active levels: plain
(해라체), panmal (반말체), familiar (하게체), semiformal (하오체), polite (해요체), formal (합쇼체), plus
an occasional seventh in certain formal writing (하소서체). **The author reports this system is
actively collapsing**: younger Seoulites no longer use the familiar or semiformal styles at all, and
since the "polite" style is mechanically just panmal + the particle 요, "the honorific inflectional
system is rapidly being reduced to only three different sets of endings: plain, panmal, and formal"
(p. 300–301).

#### Syntax — Japanese-influenced calques (7.5)

Korean syntax has stayed "relatively unchanged since the earliest records," with limited exceptions
from language contact. 보다 (pwota), a comparison particle historically shortened from Early Modern
postposition pwotaka, gained a new **adverbial** use during the Japanese colonial period (보다
위대하다 "is greater"), modeled directly on Japanese *yori*, which likewise functions as both particle
and adverb. Similarly, the construction 뿐만 아니라 ("not only that, in addition") is modeled on the
literary Japanese form *nomi-narazu* (p. 301–302).

#### Vocabulary — the neo-Sinitic coinage system (7.6)

The single largest lexical change in Contemporary Korean has been vocabulary, overwhelmingly via
**neo-Sinitic coinages** — two- or occasionally three-character Chinese compounds, often invoking a
classical literary allusion, that were mostly minted in Japan (which modernized earlier) to translate
Western concepts, then adopted pan-regionally across China, Japan, and Korea because they fit the
shared elite Sinitic lexical stock (the author compares this to Latinate coinage in European
languages) — not borrowed directly from English/Western languages themselves, since direct knowledge
of English was essentially absent in Korea at the time. Example: Fukuzawa Yukichi's 1867 coinage
文明(開化) bunmei for "civilization," from a *Book of Changes* allusion, now ordinary in both Japanese
and Korean (문명). Some productive derivational morphemes came along with this wave: **-적 (-cek)**
turns nouns into adjectives (애국 → 애국적); **-주의 (-cwuuy)** means "-ism" (이상주의, 사회주의,
자본주의). Short-lived enlightenment-era neologisms (개화- prefix compounds) mostly died out; the
longer-lived **양- "Western"** prefix (양파 "bulb onions") has partly survived while compounds like
양담배/양복/양주 now read as quaint. Sino-Korean synonyms of native words are often felt as more
elegant/elevated, sometimes functioning as de facto honorific/professional register in domains like
medicine (치아 vs. 이 vs. 이빨; 수족 vs. 손발) (p. 301–303).

#### Vocabulary — the nativist purism movement (7.6.1)

A parallel nativist counter-movement, fueled by rising linguistic nationalism and Hangul pride, arose
soon after the neo-Sinitic vocabulary wave and intensified after liberation from Japan, with
institutional backing from South Korea's Ministry of Education. Most specialist nativist coinages
(말본 for 문법 "grammar," 이름씨 for 명사 "noun," 넘보라살 for 자외선 "ultraviolet rays") "willy-nilly
ignored the nuances of language use" and largely failed to take hold, though a few survive in
specific domains (세모꼴, 셈본 in school math textbooks). The author singles out 꼴 ("shape") as a
cautionary case: reviving this archaic, respectable Middle Korean word in coinages like 세모꼴 ignores
that in contemporary speech 꼴 has become "derogatory, vulgar, and laughable" (꼴불견, 꼴사납다, 저 꼴
좀 봐라!), making such coinages land as unintentionally comic. By contrast, **organically-emerged**
native words succeeded where top-down coinage failed — e.g. 오빠 (originally a narrow Seoul-city usage,
now standard nationwide) — and post-liberation native replacements for specific Japanese loanwords
(새치기 for ywokkwotwoli, 소매치기 for suli) stuck. New-literature-era innovations included
experimental third-person pronouns alien to earlier Korean (그 as a 3rd-person pronoun, per Kim
Tongin; 궐녀/그녀/그녜 "she"), most of which "have not been adopted into speech," plus revived archaic
literary words. Since the late 20th century, a renewed nativist-naming fashion has produced native-word
personal names (가람, 빛나, 시내, 하늘, 꽃님, 슬기, 별, and — most famously — 비, the K-pop star's stage
name) and native-word business/establishment names, contrasted with Sino-Korean names still preferred
for "staid or formal" establishments (p. 303–305).

#### Vocabulary — loanword history (7.6.2)

Colonial-period Japanese loans into Korean were extensively purged after liberation, especially in
North Korea, and to a lesser but continuing degree in South Korea; most replacements use Sino-Korean
readings of a word's original Chinese characters, sometimes a native coinage. Example chain: Japanese
*obentō* → Korean 벤또 pyenttwo → native 도시락 twosilak (standard by the 1980s). Even loans mediated
through Japan from Western languages got re-adjusted toward English pronunciation over time (센타 →
센터 "center"). Some older Japanese-era loans persist regardless (우동 "udon," 신분 from Japanese
*mibun*). Contemporary loan traffic also runs in both directions across the Korea-Japan border: 탈렌트
"TV personality" (from English "talent" via the Japanese entertainment industry) and 애니메 "anime" are
recent Japan-mediated loans into Korean youth culture, while Korean 김치 has been loaned into Japanese
as *kimuchi* (replacing an earlier Japanese term, chōsen-zuke, "Korean pickles"). English loanwords are
otherwise proliferating rapidly under a described South Korean attitude of "total availability" toward
English vocabulary, including creative **"Konglish"** coinages from English elements that don't exist
as compounds in English itself (핸드폰 "hand phone" for mobile phone; 오피스텔 "office-hotel" for a
studio apartment) — the author expects most such coinages to be short-lived nonce words, though some
will become permanent (p. 305).

#### Vocabulary — other trends: acronyms and the productive mimetic system (7.6.3)

Korean productively forms East-Asian-style acronyms/abbreviations by combining the first syllables of
a compound term's constituent words (공동위원회 "joint commission" → 공위; 노동조합 "labor union" → 노조;
불고기백반 "pulgogi and rice" → 불백). The chapter closes by reaffirming mimetic/onomatopoeic vocabulary
as one of Korean's most distinctive features — richer, per the author, than in "few other languages" —
noting that reduplicated mimetic adverbials (딸랑딸랑 "jingle-jingle," 아장아장 "totteringly") can also
be converted into predicates via the suffixes **-거리다/-대다** (딸랑거리다 "[the bell] jingles,"
아장거리다 "[the baby] toddles"), a derivational pattern first attested in the textual record around
the early 19th century (p. 305).

## Copyright discipline reminder

Selective quotes + paraphrase + analysis only — never bulk reproduction of vocabulary boxes,
dialogue blocks, or explanatory prose. See `../../00_Reference_Extraction_Spec.md`. All Hanja/Hangul/
Yale-romanized forms throughout this file are preserved as short, individually-cited lexical/
grammatical items (the legitimate "vocabulary" content of a historical-linguistics source), not as
reproduced source prose; longer analytical passages from the book are paraphrased rather than quoted,
and the handful of direct quotations are sentence-length or shorter, used only where the source's own
wording (or a primary-source excerpt it quotes) is the actual evidence under discussion.
